We comprehensively evaluate 25 state-of-the-art LLMs using 5-shot chain-of-thought (CoT) and zero-shot prompting strategies, analyzing their performance across linguistic and cultural boundaries. Our experiments reveal consistent performance degradation from high-resource languages to lower-resource ones, with the best models achieving over 70% accuracy on English but dropping to around 40% for languages like Swahili, highlighting persistent gaps in multilingual capabilities despite recent advances. MMLU-ProX is an ongoing project; we are expanding our benchmark by incorporating additional languages and evaluating more language models to provide a more comprehensive assessment of multilingual capabilities.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the MMLU-ProX paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MMLU-ProX, a new multilingual benchmark designed to evaluate the reasoning abilities of large language models (LLMs) across 13 typologically diverse languages. It builds upon the MMLU-Pro benchmark by translating the same questions into multiple languages using a semi-automatic process involving LLM translation followed by expert annotation. The authors evaluate 25 state-of-the-art LLMs using both 5-shot chain-of-thought (CoT) and zero-shot prompting, revealing significant performance variations across languages and models. The results highlight a consistent performance degradation from high-resource to low-resource languages, indicating persistent gaps in multilingual reasoning capabilities. The paper emphasizes the importance of culturally and linguistically inclusive benchmark development for achieving equitable language technologies.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in creating a *high-quality* multilingual benchmark, MMLU-ProX, *based on* MMLU-Pro's challenging, reasoning-focused design. While multilingual benchmarks exist (e.g., Global MMLU), MMLU-ProX distinguishes itself through its rigorous translation pipeline (LLM translation + expert verification) and focus on maintaining the difficulty level of the original MMLU-Pro. The semi-automated translation pipeline appears novel, allowing for potentially scalable multilingual benchmark creation.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial challenge in the LLM field: the need for robust multilingual evaluation. Demonstrating the persistent performance gap between high- and low-resource languages underscores the limitations of current LLMs and highlights the importance of developing models that are truly capable of cross-lingual reasoning. By providing a challenging and well-validated benchmark, MMLU-ProX offers researchers and practitioners a valuable tool for assessing and improving the multilingual capabilities of LLMs. The scale of languages covered, the number of models evaluated, and detailed analysis adds weight to the significance. However, the authors need to validate claims that the reasoning capabilities of current models may be influenced by multiple factors simultaneously.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **High-Quality Data:**  The semi-automatic translation process with expert annotation ensures conceptual accuracy, terminological consistency, and cultural relevance, addressing a common weakness of translated benchmarks.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The evaluation includes a substantial number of state-of-the-art LLMs across multiple languages and prompting strategies, providing a detailed analysis of multilingual performance.
    *   **Focus on Reasoning:** Building upon MMLU-Pro, the benchmark emphasizes complex reasoning skills, which are essential for many real-world applications.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and organized, with clear explanations of the methodology and results.
    *   **Detailed Analysis:**  The paper provides detailed analysis of model performance across different languages, reasoning-enhancement techniques and prompting strategies
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Language Coverage:** Although covering 13 languages is a good start, the benchmark could benefit from expanding to even more diverse languages, particularly those underrepresented in existing datasets.
    *   **Cost implication:** The authors acknowledge a substantial cost. The availability of the benchmark to the research community may depend on resource availability, and scalability could be a concern.
    *   **Validation Process Ongoing:** The human verification aspect is still not complete, with potential implications for the overall dataset's integrity.
    *   **Reliance on LLMs for Translation:** While expert validation mitigates this, relying on LLMs for the initial translation may introduce biases or limitations in the target languages.

*   **Potential Influence:** MMLU-ProX has the potential to become a widely used benchmark in the LLM field, driving research toward developing more robust and equitable multilingual models. It can facilitate the identification of specific weaknesses in LLMs and guide the development of targeted training strategies.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:**

MMLU-ProX represents a *substantial* contribution to the LLM field by addressing a critical gap in multilingual evaluation. The rigorous methodology, comprehensive evaluation, and clear presentation are strong positives. However, limitations in language coverage, potentially unsustainable costs and ongoing validation process prevent a higher score. The work shows good novelty and significance, and it has the potential to meaningfully influence future research and development in the area of multilingual LLMs, justifying a score of 8.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### TokenCarve: Information-Preserving Visual Token Compression in Multimodal Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10501v1)
- **Authors**: Xudong Tan, Peng Ye, Chongjun Tu, Jianjian Cao, Yaoxin Yang, Lin Zhang, Dongzhan Zhou, Tao Chen
- **Abstract**: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) are becoming increasingly popular, while the high computational cost associated with multimodal data input, particularly from visual tokens, poses a significant challenge. Existing training-based token compression methods improve inference efficiency but require costly retraining, while training-free methods struggle to maintain performance when aggressively reducing token counts. In this study, we reveal that the performance degradation of MLLM closely correlates with the accelerated loss of information in the attention output matrix. This insight introduces a novel information-preserving perspective, making it possible to maintain performance even under extreme token compression. Based on this finding, we propose TokenCarve, a training-free, plug-and-play, two-stage token compression framework. The first stage employs an Information-Preservation-Guided Selection (IPGS) strategy to prune low-information tokens, while the second stage further leverages IPGS to guide token merging, minimizing information loss. Extensive experiments on 11 datasets and 2 model variants demonstrate the effectiveness of TokenCarve. It can even reduce the number of visual tokens to 22.2% of the original count, achieving a 1.23x speedup in inference, a 64% reduction in KV cache storage, and only a 1.54% drop in accuracy. Our code is available at https://github.com/ShawnTan86/TokenCarve.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "TokenCarve," a novel, training-free, and plug-and-play visual token compression framework for Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). It addresses the high computational cost associated with processing visual tokens. The key insight is that MLLM performance degradation during token compression is strongly correlated with the loss of information within the attention output matrix. TokenCarve employs a two-stage approach: 1) Information-Preservation-Guided Selection (IPGS) to prune low-information tokens, and 2) Token merging, further guided by IPGS to minimize information loss. Experiments across various datasets and model variants demonstrate TokenCarve's effectiveness in reducing visual token count while preserving performance, improving inference speed, and reducing KV cache storage.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in framing token compression from an "information preservation" perspective, particularly focusing on the attention output matrix.  Existing methods largely treat token compression as a reduction of redundancy without explicit consideration of how compression affects critical information flow.  The IPGS strategy and two-stage approach are novel instantiations of this principle. The use of SVD to quantify token contribution is a creative adaptation. However, techniques like token merging are not entirely new, though their application within this specific information-preserving framework is original.

*   **Significance:** The paper tackles a critical challenge in deploying MLLMs: the high computational cost.  A successful training-free compression method has significant practical implications, allowing for easier deployment of MLLMs without the need for costly retraining or model-specific adaptation. The empirical results, showcasing substantial token reduction with minimal performance degradation, underscore the practical value. The reduction in KV cache usage and the resulting speedup are also significant benefits.  The thorough evaluation across diverse datasets and model sizes adds to the credibility.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Information-Preserving Perspective:** The key strength is the shift in focus toward information retention rather than simply redundancy reduction.
    *   **Training-Free & Plug-and-Play:** The training-free nature is a major advantage, enabling easy integration with existing MLLMs.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** Extensive experiments demonstrate consistent and significant improvements across various benchmarks and models.
    *   **Clear Articulation:** The paper clearly explains the problem, motivation, proposed solution, and experimental setup.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited theoretical justification:** The selection of layer 2 in LLM might be based on observations, but it lacks a detailed theoretical backing or justification.
    *   **Computational cost:** Although the inference is faster and cache usage is less, it's not clear how the computational cost of the whole procedure, including carving stage, is compared to FastV etc.
    *   **Dependency on SVD:** The dependency on Singular Value Decomposition (SVD) may introduce computational overhead, especially for very large models. While the method claims practical acceleration, a detailed computational analysis of SVD usage during the carving stages should be further elaborated.
    *   **Incremental improvement:** While the paper claims to be the state-of-the-art, the performance gain compared to the previous works is incremental in some cases.

*   **Potential Influence:** TokenCarve has the potential to influence the field of MLLMs by providing a practical and effective method for token compression.  The information-preserving perspective could inspire new research directions in compression techniques and attention mechanisms. The plug-and-play nature could accelerate the adoption of MLLMs in resource-constrained environments.

*   **Missing information and unclear choices:** The choice of parameters, especially λ and other parameters related to GSM, could be explained in more detail, and experiments could be conducted.

*   **Concerns about scalability and efficiency:** the choice of the second layer may not scale to larger models and the current design heavily relies on Singular Value Decomposition which is quite computationally expensive.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a significant contribution with its novel information-preserving framework for visual token compression in MLLMs. The method is practical, training-free, and well-validated. The key innovation of directly addressing information loss during compression is valuable. However, the use of SVD and the choice of layer 2 raises concerns about scalability and theoretical justification, respectively. Therefore, the score reflects the practical significance and promising results while acknowledging the limitations in theoretical grounding and computational complexity. There's room for improvement in theoretically justifying design choices and optimizing computational efficiency.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### SySLLM: Generating Synthesized Policy Summaries for Reinforcement Learning Agents Using Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10509v1)
- **Authors**: Sahar Admoni, Omer Ben-Porat, Ofra Amir
- **Abstract**: Policies generated by Reinforcement Learning (RL) algorithms can be difficult to describe to users, as they result from the interplay between complex reward structures and neural network-based representations. This combination often leads to unpredictable behaviors, making policies challenging to analyze and posing significant obstacles to fostering human trust in real-world applications. Global policy summarization methods aim to describe agent behavior through a demonstration of actions in a subset of world-states. However, users can only watch a limited number of demonstrations, restricting their understanding of policies. Moreover, those methods overly rely on user interpretation, as they do not synthesize observations into coherent patterns. In this work, we present SySLLM (Synthesized Summary using LLMs), a novel method that employs synthesis summarization, utilizing large language models' (LLMs) extensive world knowledge and ability to capture patterns, to generate textual summaries of policies. Specifically, an expert evaluation demonstrates that the proposed approach generates summaries that capture the main insights generated by experts while not resulting in significant hallucinations. Additionally, a user study shows that SySLLM summaries are preferred over demonstration-based policy summaries and match or surpass their performance in objective agent identification tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SySLLM, a method for generating natural language summaries of reinforcement learning (RL) agent policies using large language models (LLMs).  The method involves first collecting agent-environment interactions, captioning observations and actions into natural language descriptions, and then prompting an LLM to synthesize a summary that captures key patterns in the agent's behavior. The authors evaluate SySLLM through expert evaluations comparing its summaries to those created by RL experts and a user study comparing SySLLM summaries to video-based summaries (HIGHLIGHTS-DIV). The results suggest that SySLLM summaries align well with expert knowledge, are preferred by users over video summaries, and achieve comparable or better performance in agent identification tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The application of LLMs for *global* RL policy summarization is relatively novel. While LLMs have been used for various tasks in RL (e.g., exploration, state abstraction, instruction following), using them to synthesize *high-level, global* textual summaries, directly interpretable by users without deep RL knowledge, is a valuable contribution. SySLLM takes a step beyond the existing demonstration-based summary methods and provides a holistic perspective on RL policies. However, the core idea leverages capabilities already inherent in LLMs – summarizing text. The novelty lies in the application to a specific problem area and the well-structured evaluation.
*   **Significance:** Explainable Reinforcement Learning (XRL) is crucial for fostering trust and facilitating the real-world deployment of RL agents. SySLLM addresses a significant challenge in XRL: the difficulty in understanding complex policies. Providing concise and interpretable summaries helps bridge the gap between the inner workings of RL agents and human understanding. The user study provides compelling evidence that people prefer natural language LLM-generated summaries and that the system can assist non-experts to better understand the agent's strategy. The rigorous evaluation, including both expert evaluations and user studies, significantly strengthens the paper's contribution. A weakness, however, is the reliance on a captioning function; though discussed, the quality of the output is obviously related to captioner performance, and the dependence on such a captioner for different environments weakens generalization claims. There are also open questions related to hallucination which, while minimal, are noted. The method could benefit from a more thorough investigation and discussion around this issue.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper is well-written, clearly structured, and easy to follow.
    *   The method is well-motivated, addressing a practical problem in XRL.
    *   The experimental evaluation is thorough, including expert and user studies.
    *   The results are promising, demonstrating the potential of SySLLM for RL policy summarization.
    * The paper recognizes some limitations and proposes reasonable directions for future work.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Reliance on captioning function: The summarization quality heavily depends on the quality of the captioning function.
    *   LLM Hallucinations: While limited, the possibility of LLM hallucinations is a concern and should be addressed more thoroughly.
    *   Limited Environments: While diverse, the environments used are still relatively simple (Minigrid).
    * The paper did not adequately compare LLM-generated summaries with expert-generated summaries, and that comparison could be improved (e.g., the authors use a recall metric).

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of 7. The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field of XRL by demonstrating the effectiveness of LLMs for generating global policy summaries.  While the core idea of using LLMs for summarization is not entirely new, the specific application to RL policy summarization, coupled with a rigorous evaluation, is a significant step forward. The primary weaknesses are the dependency on captioning and the possibility of hallucinations. These, though, are common problems in applying LLMs to new areas, and therefore not a major detraction. This work can act as a baseline and set expectations for the role of LLMs for providing insights to RL agents and behaviors.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Conformal Prediction Sets for Deep Generative Models via Reduction to Conformal Regression
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10512v1)
- **Authors**: Hooman Shahrokhi, Devjeet Raj Roy, Yan Yan, Venera Arnaoudova, Janaradhan Rao Doppa
- **Abstract**: We consider the problem of generating valid and small prediction sets by sampling outputs (e.g., software code and natural language text) from a black-box deep generative model for a given input (e.g., textual prompt). The validity of a prediction set is determined by a user-defined binary admissibility function depending on the target application. For example, requiring at least one program in the set to pass all test cases in code generation application. To address this problem, we develop a simple and effective conformal inference algorithm referred to as Generative Prediction Sets (GPS). Given a set of calibration examples and black-box access to a deep generative model, GPS can generate prediction sets with provable guarantees. The key insight behind GPS is to exploit the inherent structure within the distribution over the minimum number of samples needed to obtain an admissible output to develop a simple conformal regression approach over the minimum number of samples. Experiments on multiple datasets for code and math word problems using different large language models demonstrate the efficacy of GPS over state-of-the-art methods.
- **Summary**: This paper introduces Generative Prediction Sets (GPS), a novel conformal prediction algorithm designed to generate marginally valid prediction sets for deep generative models (DGMs) with unbounded output spaces. GPS addresses the challenge of uncertainty quantification for DGMs, particularly in safety-critical domains. The key idea is to reformulate the prediction set construction problem into estimating the number of samples needed to achieve admissible outputs, enabling selective abstention on specific examples. This is achieved by learning an auxiliary predictor to estimate the minimum number of samples required to obtain an admissible output, then using conformal regression to construct a valid prediction interval around this estimate. GPS reduces the problem to a vanilla CP problem that existing methods can address.

The paper compares GPS with Conformal Language Modeling (CLM), demonstrating that GPS achieves lower abstention rates and higher non-abstention coverage than CLM at comparable set sizes, especially at tighter confidence levels. The authors showcase the efficacy of GPS on multiple benchmark datasets for code and math word problems using different large language models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its specific application of conformal prediction to deep generative models in a way that mitigates the computational issues caused by unbounded output spaces. Reducing the complex task of set prediction with DGMs to a conformal regression problem is a significant contribution. The selective abstention based on estimating the required samples is also a novel aspect.
*   **Significance:** The significance of the work is in providing a practical and theoretically sound method for uncertainty quantification in DGMs. It addresses a crucial need in deploying DGMs in safety-critical applications where uncertainty is a concern. This addresses a problem that had been limited by requiring enumeration of the output space. The practical improvement in abstention rates and coverage also makes this relevant in domains where model output is relied upon.
*   **Strengths:** The paper is clearly written and well-structured. The algorithm is well-motivated and the theoretical guarantees are clearly stated. The empirical evaluation is extensive, covering a range of datasets and models. The comparison with CLM provides a clear understanding of the advantages of GPS.
*   **Weaknesses:** The success of GPS heavily relies on the quality of the admissibility estimator *f*. The paper acknowledges this but could benefit from a more in-depth analysis of the impact of *f*'s performance on the overall performance of GPS. Specifically, the experiments to justify these results on larger, more complex models where generating training data for *f* is more difficult.

*   **Justification for Score:** While the paper makes a valuable contribution to the field of uncertainty quantification for DGMs, it doesn't represent a radical shift in the underlying theory of conformal prediction. It uses standard CP on a regression that captures information about the underlying DGM. This limits its potential for influencing or revolutionizing the approach to the problem in a large area. Also, because the estimator, *f*, is crucial to performance, and its success relies on surrogate models when model inputs are not directly accessible, this limits performance in certain cases.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Probing LLMs for Multilingual Discourse Generalization Through a Unified Label Set
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10515v1)
- **Authors**: Florian Eichin, Yang Janet Liu, Barbara Plank, Michael A. Hedderich
- **Abstract**: Discourse understanding is essential for many NLP tasks, yet most existing work remains constrained by framework-dependent discourse representations. This work investigates whether large language models (LLMs) capture discourse knowledge that generalizes across languages and frameworks. We address this question along two dimensions: (1) developing a unified discourse relation label set to facilitate cross-lingual and cross-framework discourse analysis, and (2) probing LLMs to assess whether they encode generalizable discourse abstractions. Using multilingual discourse relation classification as a testbed, we examine a comprehensive set of 23 LLMs of varying sizes and multilingual capabilities. Our results show that LLMs, especially those with multilingual training corpora, can generalize discourse information across languages and frameworks. Further layer-wise analyses reveal that language generalization at the discourse level is most salient in the intermediate layers. Lastly, our error analysis provides an account of challenging relation classes.
- **Summary**: This paper addresses the important issue of discourse generalization across languages and frameworks, a relatively underexplored area in NLP. The authors develop a unified discourse relation label set and probe a range of large language models (LLMs) to assess their ability to encode generalizable discourse abstractions. Their experiments, using the multilingual DISRPT benchmark, indicate that LLMs, particularly those with multilingual training, can indeed generalize discourse information.  The paper also includes layer-wise analyses showing the importance of intermediate layers for language generalization at the discourse level, and an error analysis providing insight into challenging relation classes.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its comprehensive probing of LLMs for cross-lingual and cross-framework discourse generalization using a *unified* label set. While previous work has explored discourse in LLMs, it often relies on framework-dependent datasets or is limited to monolingual settings.  Developing and applying a unified label set to the multilingual DISRPT dataset *is* a significant contribution. However, the probing techniques themselves (attention-based and linear classification) are not particularly novel.  Other studies have used attention weights and simple classifiers to probe representations, though this is the first time the *combination* of these methods has been applied *at scale* in the cross-framework/cross-lingual discourse setting.

*   **Significance:** The findings are significant for several reasons: (1) They suggest that LLMs possess some degree of language-agnostic discourse understanding, which is crucial for building more robust and generalizable NLP systems. (2)  The paper provides empirical evidence for the impact of multilingual training on discourse generalization capabilities of LLMs. (3) The identification of challenging relation classes provides directions for future research on improving discourse modeling.  The observation about the intermediate layers is also interesting and offers a potential explanation as to why LLMs work across languages. However, the "probing" approach provides *correlational* evidence not *causal* evidence. So these results have to be interpreted with some caution. Also, the unified label set while enabling comparability also *simplifies* discourse relations which means there is some information lost in the process.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Comprehensive experimental design, covering a wide range of LLMs and languages.
    *   Creation and application of a unified discourse relation label set, enabling cross-framework comparison.
    *   Clear presentation of results and insightful analyses (layer-wise and error analysis).
    *   The paper addresses a gap in the literature by investigating discourse generalization rather than focusing on specific frameworks or languages.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The probing methodology is relatively standard.  The core novelty is the dataset preparation and application of standard techniques to a new problem area.
    *   The choice of a simple linear classifier as a probe might limit the ability to capture complex discourse representations encoded in LLMs.  More sophisticated probing techniques could reveal additional insights.
    *   The unified label set, while useful for generalization, inevitably simplifies the nuances of individual discourse frameworks, potentially obscuring certain language-specific or theory-specific phenomena.
    *   The paper lacks a deeper theoretical analysis of why certain relations are more challenging for LLMs to generalize.
    *   It would be helpful to see if these models are good at downstream tasks that rely on an understanding of discourse relations. The probing experiments are interesting but the proof is in the pudding and it's hard to assess from this paper if these models actually have a good understanding of discourse phenomena.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper is likely to stimulate further research on discourse generalization in LLMs.  It provides a valuable benchmark (the unified DISRPT dataset and label set) and identifies key factors influencing generalization (multilingual training, model size, and specific relation classes).  The paper can also inform the development of more effective pre-training strategies for LLMs that aim to improve their discourse understanding capabilities. The work highlights the need for more balanced multilingual discourse resources.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**  The paper makes a solid, important, and *incremental* contribution. It addresses a relevant problem and delivers insights based on comprehensive experiments. While the technical novelty of the probing methods is limited, the application of these methods *at scale* to the problem of cross-lingual/cross-framework discourse generalizability, combined with the effort to create a unified label set, justifies a score of 7.  A higher score would require more innovative probing techniques, a deeper theoretical analysis, and evidence showing that these models are good at some downstream discourse-level tasks. Also more careful attention to some of the potential simplification problems of the unified dataset might lead to some interesting insights.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### PiSA: A Self-Augmented Data Engine and Training Strategy for 3D Understanding with Large Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10529v1)
- **Authors**: Zilu Guo, Hongbin Lin, Zhihao Yuan, Chaoda Zheng, Pengshuo Qiu, Dongzhi Jiang, Renrui Zhang, Chun-Mei Feng, Zhen Li
- **Abstract**: 3D Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have recently made substantial advancements. However, their potential remains untapped, primarily due to the limited quantity and suboptimal quality of 3D datasets. Current approaches attempt to transfer knowledge from 2D MLLMs to expand 3D instruction data, but still face modality and domain gaps. To this end, we introduce PiSA-Engine (Point-Self-Augmented-Engine), a new framework for generating instruction point-language datasets enriched with 3D spatial semantics. We observe that existing 3D MLLMs offer a comprehensive understanding of point clouds for annotation, while 2D MLLMs excel at cross-validation by providing complementary information. By integrating holistic 2D and 3D insights from off-the-shelf MLLMs, PiSA-Engine enables a continuous cycle of high-quality data generation. We select PointLLM as the baseline and adopt this co-evolution training framework to develop an enhanced 3D MLLM, termed PointLLM-PiSA. Additionally, we identify limitations in previous 3D benchmarks, which often feature coarse language captions and insufficient category diversity, resulting in inaccurate evaluations. To address this gap, we further introduce PiSA-Bench, a comprehensive 3D benchmark covering six key aspects with detailed and diverse labels. Experimental results demonstrate PointLLM-PiSA's state-of-the-art performance in zero-shot 3D object captioning and generative classification on our PiSA-Bench, achieving significant improvements of 46.45% (+8.33%) and 63.75% (+16.25%), respectively. We will release the code, datasets, and benchmark.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "PiSA: A Self-Augmented Data Engine and Training Strategy for 3D Understanding with Large Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PiSA (Point-Self-Augmented-Engine), a novel framework for improving 3D Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). PiSA addresses the limited quantity and quality of existing 3D datasets by employing a three-stage process: (1) 3D-space data annotation using 3D MLLMs to extract depth and spatial information, (2) 2D-space data refinement using 2D MLLMs to cross-validate and enrich appearance and detail descriptions while preserving 3D spatial information, and (3) Iterative 3D data bootstrap for continuous high-quality data generation through a co-evolution training strategy. The paper then presents PointLLM-PiSA, a baseline model enhanced using the PiSA-Engine. Finally, the work introduces PiSA-Bench, a comprehensive 3D benchmark designed to address the limitations of existing benchmarks with finer-grained labels across six key aspects: general description, color, shape, count, spatial relations, usage, and hallucination. Experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of PiSA-Engine and PointLLM-PiSA in zero-shot 3D object captioning and generative classification, achieving significant performance improvements on PiSA-Bench.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a self-augmentation pipeline (PiSA-Engine) for 3D data, which appears reasonably novel. The idea of leveraging both 2D and 3D MLLMs in a complementary manner to generate high-quality 3D data is interesting and addresses a key bottleneck in the field. Iterative refinement of data with the integration of both 2D and 3D perspectives is also innovative. Additionally, the introduction of PiSA-Bench, a comprehensive benchmark addressing limitations of previous ones, adds significant value. However, the iterative self-training approach builds upon existing work in 2D MLLMs, reducing the novelty of PiSA-Engine. Additionally, the training loop for PointLLM-PiSA can be considered as an adaptation of ideas in self-training and knowledge distillation in the domain of 3D scene understanding.

*   **Significance:** Addressing the problem of limited data and suboptimal quality in 3D understanding is highly relevant. The advancements in 3D MLLMs hinge on the availability of high-quality 3D training datasets, and PiSA offers a potential solution for scaling such datasets.  The PiSA-Bench contributes a valuable resource for the community, facilitating more accurate and comprehensive evaluations of 3D MLLMs. The experimental results also suggest real performance gains, validating the effectiveness of PiSA-Engine. The release of the code, datasets, and benchmark would further increase the paper's impact.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Addresses a critical bottleneck in the 3D MLLM field.
    *   The PiSA-Engine framework is well-motivated and clearly explained.
    *   Introduction of a comprehensive benchmark for more accurate evaluations.
    *   Demonstrated performance improvements on challenging tasks.
    *   Plug-and-play framework to show extensive scalability.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The approach builds upon well-established self-training techniques, which limits its novelty.
    *   While the experimental results showcase gains, a deeper analysis of the types of errors corrected or introduced by PiSA-Engine would be beneficial.
    *   The PiSA-Bench construction methodology requires access to GPT-4, potentially hindering its accessibility for some researchers. Also, using a closed model for benchmark creation introduces a level of opaqueness regarding how the labels are generated.

**Justification:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of 3D MLLMs by addressing the data scarcity and quality issues. While the underlying techniques are not entirely groundbreaking, the combination of 2D and 3D MLLMs in a carefully designed framework (PiSA-Engine), along with the creation of a comprehensive benchmark (PiSA-Bench), makes the work significant. The experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed approach, making the paper relevant to researchers and practitioners in 3D computer vision and NLP. The plug-and-play framework also makes the solution attractive, allowing it to scale in a wider context.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### KUDA: Keypoints to Unify Dynamics Learning and Visual Prompting for Open-Vocabulary Robotic Manipulation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10546v1)
- **Authors**: Zixian Liu, Mingtong Zhang, Yunzhu Li
- **Abstract**: With the rapid advancement of large language models (LLMs) and vision-language models (VLMs), significant progress has been made in developing open-vocabulary robotic manipulation systems. However, many existing approaches overlook the importance of object dynamics, limiting their applicability to more complex, dynamic tasks. In this work, we introduce KUDA, an open-vocabulary manipulation system that integrates dynamics learning and visual prompting through keypoints, leveraging both VLMs and learning-based neural dynamics models. Our key insight is that a keypoint-based target specification is simultaneously interpretable by VLMs and can be efficiently translated into cost functions for model-based planning. Given language instructions and visual observations, KUDA first assigns keypoints to the RGB image and queries the VLM to generate target specifications. These abstract keypoint-based representations are then converted into cost functions, which are optimized using a learned dynamics model to produce robotic trajectories. We evaluate KUDA on a range of manipulation tasks, including free-form language instructions across diverse object categories, multi-object interactions, and deformable or granular objects, demonstrating the effectiveness of our framework. The project page is available at http://kuda-dynamics.github.io.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces KUDA, an open-vocabulary robotic manipulation system that unifies dynamics learning and visual prompting using keypoints. The core idea is to leverage keypoints as a shared representation between vision-language models (VLMs) and dynamics models.  KUDA takes language instructions and RGBD observations as input.  It samples keypoints, uses a VLM to generate code specifying target configurations based on those keypoints, translates these specifications into cost functions, and optimizes them using a learned dynamics model for model-based planning. The system is evaluated on a range of manipulation tasks involving different object categories, including multi-object interactions and deformable objects. The results show the effectiveness of KUDA in handling complex and dynamic tasks compared to existing approaches. The paper also presents a prompt retriever to improve VLM performance through few-shot learning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of unifying dynamics learning and visual prompting through keypoints is a significant contribution. While previous works have explored VLMs for robotics and dynamics learning separately, this paper effectively bridges the gap by using keypoints as an intermediate representation. This allows the system to leverage the strengths of both approaches: the flexibility of VLMs in understanding language instructions and the accuracy of dynamics models in predicting object behaviors. The prompt retriever is a nice addition that improves VLM performance.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial limitation of many existing open-vocabulary robotic manipulation systems, which often overlook object dynamics and are limited to rigid objects and coarse-grained manipulation. KUDA's ability to handle complex and dynamic tasks, such as manipulating ropes and granular objects, expands the applicability of robotic systems to a wider range of real-world scenarios. The paper demonstrates that incorporating dynamics learning can significantly improve the performance of open-vocabulary manipulation.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly defines the problem of open-vocabulary robotic manipulation and the limitations of existing approaches.
    *   **Novel Approach:** The use of keypoints as a unified representation is a novel and effective way to integrate VLMs and dynamics learning.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The system is evaluated on a range of manipulation tasks with different object categories and language instructions.
    *   **Detailed Error Analysis:** The paper includes a detailed error analysis, providing insights into the failure cases of the system and identifying areas for future improvement.
    * **Prompt Retriever:** The inclusion of a prompt retriever allows KUDA to adapt to new tasks efficiently.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Sim-to-Real Gap:** The dynamics models are trained in simulation, which can lead to a sim-to-real gap and limit the system's generalization to different object categories. This is a common limitation in robotics research.
    *   **Perception Limitations:** The perception module is a significant source of error, particularly in detecting objects and tracking keypoints in dense object piles.
    *   **Top Camera:** Use of only a top camera restricts the ability to understand 3D environments.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of open-vocabulary robotic manipulation. The keypoint-based unified representation could be adopted by other researchers to develop more robust and versatile robotic systems. The paper's findings highlight the importance of incorporating dynamics learning into open-vocabulary manipulation and could inspire future research in this area.
*   **Justification:** While the core concept is strong and the evaluation is solid, the sim-to-real transfer and reliance on a top-down view are significant limitations. The prompt retrieval system seems effective, though further analysis would strengthen this aspect.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 7/10

### Short-term AI literacy intervention does not reduce over-reliance on incorrect ChatGPT recommendations
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10556v1)
- **Authors**: Brett Puppart, Jaan Aru
- **Abstract**: In this study, we examined whether a short-form AI literacy intervention could reduce the adoption of incorrect recommendations from large language models. High school seniors were randomly assigned to either a control or an intervention group, which received an educational text explaining ChatGPT's working mechanism, limitations, and proper use. Participants solved math puzzles with the help of ChatGPT's recommendations, which were incorrect in half of the cases. Results showed that students adopted incorrect suggestions 52.1% of the time, indicating widespread over-reliance. The educational intervention did not significantly reduce over-reliance. Instead, it led to an increase in ignoring ChatGPT's correct recommendations. We conclude that the usage of ChatGPT is associated with over-reliance and it is not trivial to increase AI literacy to counter over-reliance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary of the paper and a critical evaluation with a score:

**Summary:**

This study investigated whether a short AI literacy intervention could reduce over-reliance on incorrect recommendations from ChatGPT among high school seniors. Students were randomly assigned to either a control group or an intervention group that received an educational text explaining ChatGPT's workings, limitations, and proper use. Participants then solved math puzzles with the help of ChatGPT, where half of the recommendations were intentionally incorrect. The results indicated significant over-reliance on ChatGPT's suggestions, with students adopting incorrect recommendations over half the time. However, the AI literacy intervention *failed* to significantly reduce over-reliance and surprisingly led to increased under-reliance on *correct* recommendations. The authors conclude that overcoming over-reliance on ChatGPT is not trivial and might require more than a short-form intervention.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:** The paper addresses a relevant and timely issue: the potential for over-reliance on large language models in educational settings. The experimental design, while not perfect, attempts to measure the effectiveness of a targeted intervention. The study's focus on high school seniors is also valuable, as this demographic is increasingly likely to interact with AI tools. The paper clearly identifies a problem, which is the high acceptance rate of incorrect solutions offered by ChatGPT.

*   **Weaknesses:** The study's sample size (n=36) is small, limiting the generalizability of the findings. The intervention was short-form, which may not be sufficient to change established cognitive habits. The study focused on only one type of task (math puzzles), which may not be representative of how students use ChatGPT in other contexts. The "increased under-reliance" is puzzling, and while the authors offer some explanations, it requires further investigation to fully understand the underlying reasons. There's a lack of discussion of potential confounding variables, such as pre-existing math abilities or technology familiarity. The study only measures over-reliance directly, and doesn't have an additional assessment of the comprehension of the intervention material beyond the self-referential statements (which is a somewhat weak measure). It's unclear how representative the selected ChatGPT recommendations are and if the chosen incorrect answers were convincingly incorrect.

*   **Novelty and Significance:** While the topic is timely, the results are somewhat underwhelming. The *failure* of the intervention is perhaps the most important finding, suggesting that a more robust approach to AI literacy is needed. However, the methodology and intervention themselves are not exceptionally novel. The finding that over-reliance is still common is incremental but confirms the importance of the research question. It suggests the field should move towards more effective interventions, rather than simply demonstrating the problem.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's finding has a moderate influence in the field, as it offers evidence to be wary about brief intervention approaches to address over-reliance on AI. It highlights the difficulty in changing user behavior and prompts further research on the design of effective AI literacy programs and the development of strategies that promote critical thinking skills. It serves as a caution for educators and policymakers who are considering integrating LLMs into the classroom.

**Rigorous Rationale:** The paper addresses a significant and relevant problem. The experiment has notable weaknesses including a small sample size and the nature of the chosen tasks and intervention. The results are somewhat unexciting, as they only show that a brief intervention does not solve over-reliance on AI, which is an important insight. However, due to a lack of novelty in the method, the incremental impact in the field, and the methodology weakness, it does not rank highly as a contribution.

**Score: 5**

- **Classification**: cs.CY
- **Score**: 5/10

### ASIDE: Architectural Separation of Instructions and Data in Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10566v1)
- **Authors**: Egor Zverev, Evgenii Kortukov, Alexander Panfilov, Soroush Tabesh, Alexandra Volkova, Sebastian Lapuschkin, Wojciech Samek, Christoph H. Lampert
- **Abstract**: Despite their remarkable performance, large language models lack elementary safety features, and this makes them susceptible to numerous malicious attacks. In particular, previous work has identified the absence of an intrinsic separation between instructions and data as a root cause for the success of prompt injection attacks. In this work, we propose an architectural change, ASIDE, that allows the model to clearly separate between instructions and data by using separate embeddings for them. Instead of training the embeddings from scratch, we propose a method to convert an existing model to ASIDE form by using two copies of the original model's embeddings layer, and applying an orthogonal rotation to one of them. We demonstrate the effectiveness of our method by showing (1) highly increased instruction-data separation scores without a loss in model capabilities and (2) competitive results on prompt injection benchmarks, even without dedicated safety training. Additionally, we study the working mechanism behind our method through an analysis of model representations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ASIDE (Architecturally Separated Instruction-Data Embeddings), a new architectural modification for large language models (LLMs) designed to enhance the separation between instructions and data.  The core idea is to assign each input token one of two distinct embeddings based on whether the token is part of an instruction or data, unlike standard LLMs that use a single embedding. This is achieved by creating a new embedding layer from two copies of the original embedding matrix and applying an orthogonal rotation to one.  Experiments demonstrate that ASIDE models achieve significantly higher instruction-data separation scores (as measured by the SEP benchmark) compared to standard fine-tuning, while maintaining competitive performance on benchmarks like AlpacaEval and demonstrating robustness to certain prompt injection attacks. The paper also includes analyses of model representations to understand the mechanism behind ASIDE's improved separation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The key idea of explicitly separating instruction and data at the embedding level is reasonably novel.  While prior works like ISE also modify embeddings to induce an instruction hierarchy, ASIDE's per-token, role-specific embeddings and the use of orthogonal rotation are distinct. The method is designed to be easily integrated into existing LLMs with minimal retraining, adding to its practical novelty.

*   **Significance:**  The problem of prompt injection and the general lack of instruction-data separation in LLMs is a critical safety concern. Addressing this issue is crucial for deploying LLMs in real-world applications, especially where they interact with untrusted user input or process external data.  Therefore, a successful solution in this area holds significant value.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition and Solution:** The paper clearly articulates the problem and presents a well-defined architectural modification.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The experimental results consistently show that ASIDE improves instruction-data separation without significantly sacrificing utility (language modelling performance).  The prompt injection experiments, although limited, suggest increased robustness.
    *   **Interpretability Analysis:**  The analysis of model representations provides some insight into how ASIDE achieves better separation, although more detailed analysis could be done.
    *   **Ease of Integration:** The proposed method to integrate ASIDE into pre-trained models without retraining from scratch is a practical advantage.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Prompt Injection Evaluation:** While the paper demonstrates ASIDE improves separation scores, the prompt injection experiments don't show consistently better robustness across *all* attacks.  The results are somewhat mixed in the indirect prompt injection experiments. Deeper exploration on other safety benchmarks might be needed.

    *   **Incremental Improvement:** The orthogonal rotation approach, while practical, may represent an incremental advance rather than a revolutionary leap in LLM architecture.
    *   **Scope of the Analysis:** While the paper performs experiments on Llama models, it would be more convincing if it had shown results on more architecture such as GPT or Claude, thus showing that the method is not architecture-specific.
    *   **Lack of Ablations:** The work could be strengthened by including experiments that look at ablations for the layer the orthogonal transformation occurs, and how important the initialization is to its success.
    *   **Overclaim on Safety:** Although encouraging, the paper's claims that ASIDE boosts models' robustness to prompt injections are somewhat strong, and the model does not always outperform fine-tuning.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence the design of safer LLM architectures. The idea of explicitly separating instructions and data could inspire other researchers to explore similar architectural modifications or training techniques. ASIDE could serve as a baseline for future research on mitigating prompt injection attacks.

*   **Justification:** While the paper offers a promising direction for improving LLM safety, the relatively mixed results on prompt injection benchmarks and the somewhat incremental nature of the architectural change limit its overall impact.
* **Broader impact and societal implications:** The paper does not address societal implications, such as how it addresses biases, misuse and so on.

**Score: 6.5**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a reasonably novel approach to an important problem in LLM safety. The empirical results demonstrate that ASIDE enhances instruction-data separation, and the interpretability analysis provides some understanding of its mechanism. While ASIDE does not consistently dominate in prompt injection robustness, it suggests the promise of using different embeddings for instructions and data. The potential for ease of integration into existing models adds to its practical value. However, the incremental nature of the change, coupled with inconsistent prompt injection results and lack of broad experimental and ablation analyses, constrain its overall significance and therefore leads to the score of 6.5.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Autoregressive Image Generation with Randomized Parallel Decoding
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10568v1)
- **Authors**: Haopeng Li, Jinyue Yang, Guoqi Li, Huan Wang
- **Abstract**: We introduce ARPG, a novel visual autoregressive model that enables randomized parallel generation, addressing the inherent limitations of conventional raster-order approaches, which hinder inference efficiency and zero-shot generalization due to their sequential, predefined token generation order. Our key insight is that effective random-order modeling necessitates explicit guidance for determining the position of the next predicted token. To this end, we propose a novel guided decoding framework that decouples positional guidance from content representation, encoding them separately as queries and key-value pairs. By directly incorporating this guidance into the causal attention mechanism, our approach enables fully random-order training and generation, eliminating the need for bidirectional attention. Consequently, ARPG readily generalizes to zero-shot tasks such as image inpainting, outpainting, and resolution expansion. Furthermore, it supports parallel inference by concurrently processing multiple queries using a shared KV cache. On the ImageNet-1K 256 benchmark, our approach attains an FID of 1.94 with only 64 sampling steps, achieving over a 20-fold increase in throughput while reducing memory consumption by over 75% compared to representative recent autoregressive models at a similar scale.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Autoregressive Image Generation with Randomized Parallel Decoding":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ARPG (Autoregressive Image Generation with Randomized Parallel Generation), a novel autoregressive model for image synthesis.  The key idea is to overcome the limitations of traditional raster-scan order autoregressive models, which are inefficient and struggle with zero-shot generalization (like inpainting).  ARPG achieves this by enabling training and inference in fully random token orders using a "guided decoding" framework. The authors decouple positional guidance from content representation by encoding them separately as queries and key-value pairs. This allows the model to predict tokens at arbitrary positions within a randomly ordered sequence.  By incorporating this guidance into the causal attention mechanism, they achieve parallel inference by processing multiple queries concurrently using a shared KV cache. The paper demonstrates competitive results on ImageNet benchmarks, achieving high quality with faster throughput and reduced memory consumption, while also showing promise in controllable generation and zero-shot tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper introduces a reasonably novel approach to autoregressive image generation. The key novelty lies in the "guided decoding" framework and the decoupling of positional guidance from content representation.  While other works like MaskGIT and RandAR have attempted to address the limitations of raster-scan order, ARPG's approach is distinct in its explicit modeling of positional guidance and the ability to maintain causality while enabling parallel decoding with KV caching.  However, the individual components are not entirely new (e.g., rotary positional embeddings are well-established). The innovation is in the specific combination and design that enables the desired properties.

**Significance:**

The significance of the work stems from its potential to improve the efficiency and generalization capabilities of autoregressive image models. The speed-up in inference is substantial, and the ability to perform zero-shot tasks without re-training is a desirable feature.  By making AR models more efficient, the work could contribute to making high-resolution image synthesis more practical. The results shown in the paper are promising and competitive with existing methods. The gains in speed and memory compared to LlamaGen and VAR are significant. The controllable generation results are also important.
The paper may be further significance by showing random-order generation capability without increasing the memory and calculation cost.

**Strengths:**

*   **Efficiency:** The paper convincingly demonstrates significant improvements in throughput and memory consumption compared to existing AR models.
*   **Generalization:** The demonstrated zero-shot capabilities are valuable and expand the usability of the model.
*   **Clear Explanation:** The paper provides a clear and well-structured explanation of the proposed method and its underlying rationale.
*   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The paper presents a comprehensive set of experiments across different benchmarks and model scales, providing strong evidence for the effectiveness of ARPG.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental Improvement:** While the "guided decoding" framework is innovative, it is built upon existing concepts like rotary position embeddings and causal attention. The contribution is more of an engineering achievement than a fundamental breakthrough.
*   **Complexity:** The method introduces added complexity in the attention mechanism. It may require more implementation effort.
*   **Limited Scope:** The paper focuses primarily on image generation and only touches upon controllable generation and zero-shot tasks. A more in-depth exploration of these capabilities would strengthen the paper. The zero-shot abilities are largely demonstrated and not critically explored in detail.
*   **Reliance on VQ-VAE/Codebooks:** Like many autoregressive image generation methods, ARPG relies on a discrete latent space learned by a VQ-VAE or similar method. The quality of the generated images is inherently limited by the quality of the VQ-VAE and the information loss during the encoding/decoding process.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to influence future research in autoregressive image generation by popularizing the idea of decoupling positional guidance from content representation and enabling parallel decoding with KV caching. The efficiency gains demonstrated in the paper could inspire further work on optimizing autoregressive models for high-resolution image synthesis.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and well-executed approach that addresses a significant challenge in autoregressive image generation. The experimental results demonstrate competitive performance and significant improvements in efficiency and memory utilization. While the method is not a groundbreaking paradigm shift, it represents a valuable contribution that could have a positive impact on the field.  The weaknesses are primarily related to the scope of the experiments and a lack of truly revolutionary elements. However, the efficiency gains and zero-shot generalization aspects make ARPG a notable advance.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Radar: Fast Long-Context Decoding for Any Transformer
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10571v1)
- **Authors**: Yongchang Hao, Mengyao Zhai, Hossein Hajimirsadeghi, Sepidehsadat Hosseini, Frederick Tung
- **Abstract**: Transformer models have demonstrated exceptional performance across a wide range of applications. Though forming the foundation of Transformer models, the dot-product attention does not scale well to long-context data since its time requirement grows quadratically with context length. In this work, we propose Radar, a training-free approach that accelerates inference by dynamically searching for the most important context tokens. For any pre-trained Transformer, Radar can reduce the decoding time complexity without training or heuristically evicting tokens. Moreover, we provide theoretical justification for our approach, demonstrating that Radar can reliably identify the most important tokens with high probability. We conduct extensive comparisons with the previous methods on a wide range of tasks. The results demonstrate that Radar achieves the state-of-the-art performance across different architectures with reduced time complexity, offering a practical solution for efficient long-context processing of Transformers.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "RADAR: FAST LONG-CONTEXT DECODING FOR ANY TRANSFORMER":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Radar, a training-free method to accelerate inference for Transformer models when dealing with long-context data.  Radar dynamically selects important context tokens by grouping tokens into segments, creating a "summarization representation" for each group using random projections, and then focusing attention on the most relevant segments. The authors provide a theoretical justification demonstrating that Radar reliably identifies important tokens.  Experiments across various tasks and architectures show that Radar achieves state-of-the-art performance with reduced time complexity compared to existing methods like StreamingLLM, Landmark Attention, H2O, and SnapKV. The method extends the context length of pre-trained transformers and reduces the decoding complexity to O(t^1.5).

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its training-free approach to dynamic context selection using random projections and hierarchical segmenting.  While techniques like kernelization and token eviction exist, Radar distinguishes itself by offering a theoretically grounded method that doesn't require fine-tuning, making it immediately applicable to any pre-trained Transformer. Random projections are not a novel idea but their use to create summarizations of context segments for the purpose of speeding up inference for long context is novel. The dynamic restructuring scheme to maintain performance without a large overhead, while simple, contributes to the practical value of the method.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical limitation of Transformers: their computational cost scales quadratically with context length. The proposed Radar addresses this with a relatively low computational overhead when the performance and ease of implementation are taken into consideration. It provides a more practical way to handle long contexts than fine-tuning attention approximations or relying on heuristic token eviction. The demonstrated performance gains across different models and tasks underscore Radar's broad applicability. The theoretical justification is a strong point and gives confidence in the method's reliability.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Training-free:** Radar's training-free nature is a significant advantage, enabling immediate deployment on existing models without additional training costs.
    *   **Theoretical Justification:** The theoretical analysis provides a solid foundation for the method, supporting its ability to identify important tokens.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The experiments comprehensively compare Radar with several existing methods and show consistent performance improvements across different tasks and architectures. The ablation studies and in-depth analyses offer insights into the method's behavior and parameter sensitivity.
    *   **Practical:** The method directly tackles a major practical issue: scaling transformers to large contexts, offering a real speed up to large models without much overhead.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Complexity:** While Radar reduces the complexity, it doesn't achieve linear complexity like some kernelization methods. O(t^1.5) can still be a bottleneck for extremely long contexts.
    *   **Ablation studies** The comparison of ablation studies lacks a proper benchmark - while the reported success rate are considerable higher that the performance of the baselines they are compared to, a formal success metric of how often the approximations find the same performance of the vanilla performance could be a good comparison to measure how close to the optimal result it is.
    *   **Memory requirements** Maintaining segment representation requires additional memory that could be significant with very large long contexts. While addressed as future work, it is important to note as one weakness of the study.
    *   **Parameter tuning.** While theoretically sound, the model has hyper-parameters n and k which the results were shown to be improved with an optimization of them. This adds an aspect of human factor into the optimization of the algorithm.

*   **Potential Influence:** Radar has the potential to significantly impact the application of Transformers in domains requiring long-context processing, such as document summarization, code generation, and scientific literature analysis. Its training-free nature and solid theoretical backing make it a practical and attractive solution for researchers and practitioners. Future work may focus on combining Radar with other efficiency techniques (e.g. FlashAttention) to achieve even better performance.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:**

Radar offers a novel and practical solution to the long-context problem in Transformers, backed by theoretical analysis and strong empirical results. Its training-free nature is a key strength. While it doesn't achieve linear complexity and has moderate memory requirements, its O(t^1.5) complexity and its ease of integration are a good trade off between performance and ease of implementation. Radar could encourage broader adoption of transformer models in tasks with large amounts of data when a model must focus on a small subset of the data when computing the attention. Therefore, it is a strong contribution that will positively impact the field.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Unveiling the Mathematical Reasoning in DeepSeek Models: A Comparative Study of Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10573v1)
- **Authors**: Afrar Jahin, Arif Hassan Zidan, Yu Bao, Shizhe Liang, Tianming Liu, Wei Zhang
- **Abstract**: With the rapid evolution of Artificial Intelligence (AI), Large Language Models (LLMs) have reshaped the frontiers of various fields, spanning healthcare, public health, engineering, science, agriculture, education, arts, humanities, and mathematical reasoning. Among these advancements, DeepSeek models have emerged as noteworthy contenders, demonstrating promising capabilities that set them apart from their peers. While previous studies have conducted comparative analyses of LLMs, few have delivered a comprehensive evaluation of mathematical reasoning across a broad spectrum of LLMs. In this work, we aim to bridge this gap by conducting an in-depth comparative study, focusing on the strengths and limitations of DeepSeek models in relation to their leading counterparts. In particular, our study systematically evaluates the mathematical reasoning performance of two DeepSeek models alongside five prominent LLMs across three independent benchmark datasets. The findings reveal several key insights: 1). DeepSeek-R1 consistently achieved the highest accuracy on two of the three datasets, demonstrating strong mathematical reasoning capabilities. 2). The distilled variant of LLMs significantly underperformed compared to its peers, highlighting potential drawbacks in using distillation techniques. 3). In terms of response time, Gemini 2.0 Flash demonstrated the fastest processing speed, outperforming other models in efficiency, which is a crucial factor for real-time applications. Beyond these quantitative assessments, we delve into how architecture, training, and optimization impact LLMs' mathematical reasoning. Moreover, our study goes beyond mere performance comparison by identifying key areas for future advancements in LLM-driven mathematical reasoning. This research enhances our understanding of LLMs' mathematical reasoning and lays the groundwork for future advancements
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a comparative study of the mathematical reasoning capabilities of several Large Language Models (LLMs), with a particular focus on DeepSeek models.  The authors evaluate DeepSeek-R1 and a distilled version, DeepSeek-1.5B, alongside prominent LLMs like Gemini 2.0 Flash, GPT-4o, and several OpenAI models (01-mini, 01, and o3-mini) across three benchmark datasets: MATH, GSM8K, and MMLU. The study analyzes accuracy and response time, examining the impact of architecture, training techniques (particularly GRPO), and distillation on mathematical reasoning performance. Key findings indicate that DeepSeek-R1 often achieves high accuracy, but distilled versions perform worse, highlighting a trade-off between efficiency and reasoning ability. Gemini 2.0 Flash stands out for its fast response time.  The paper also provides qualitative analysis of reasoning steps and identifies strengths and weaknesses of different models in specific mathematical domains (e.g., Formal Logic, Abstract Algebra).

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**

    * **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper provides a relatively comprehensive empirical evaluation across multiple LLMs and benchmark datasets. The inclusion of response time as a metric is valuable.
    * **Focus on DeepSeek:** The focus on DeepSeek models and the analysis of their architecture (GRPO) and distilled variants are useful, providing insight into the impact of specific design choices.  The study delves deeper than merely presenting performance numbers; it attempts to attribute performance differences to underlying architectural and training methodologies.
    * **Qualitative Analysis:**  The inclusion of qualitative analysis of reasoning steps from different models adds depth to the quantitative results and helps in understanding the models' reasoning processes. The comparison in Figures 4, 6 and 8 were well described.
    * **Identifies Trade-offs:** The paper clearly highlights the trade-off between model size, distillation, computational efficiency, and mathematical reasoning ability, a crucial consideration in LLM development.
* **Weaknesses:**

    * **Limited Novelty:** While the empirical comparison is valuable, the overall novelty is somewhat limited. Comparative studies of LLMs are common. The insights regarding distillation's impact on reasoning are not entirely new, even if further substantiated by this study's results.
    * **Dataset limitations:** Focusing solely on well-known benchmarks limits the study's scope. Introducing new, more targeted mathematical reasoning benchmarks would have increased novelty.
    * **Limited Model Scope**: The evaluation does not include more recent state-of-the-art models, for instance, Llama-3 and Claude, which are notable competitors. By omitting these, the work misses potential insights into the landscape of LLM capabilities.
    * **Limited Ablation of GRPO**: While they mention and attribute part of the Deepseek performance to GRPO, there is little exploration or analysis of the effect this had on performance in the work.
    * **Lack of Deeper Architectural Investigation:** While the paper touches on architectural considerations, a more in-depth analysis of how specific architectural elements impact reasoning (e.g., attention mechanisms, number of layers) would have been beneficial.

* **Significance:**

    * The paper contributes to the growing body of knowledge on LLM capabilities and limitations in mathematical reasoning.
    * It provides practical insights for developers seeking to build efficient and accurate mathematical problem-solving systems.
    * The emphasis on the DeepSeek architecture and GRPO contributes to the discussion on effective training techniques for reasoning tasks.

**Justification for the Score:**

I assign a score of **6**.

While the paper provides a comprehensive evaluation and valuable insights into DeepSeek models, it lacks significant novelty. Comparative studies of LLMs are relatively common, and the core findings regarding the trade-offs between efficiency and reasoning, while important, are not groundbreaking.  The analysis is generally sound, but could have benefited from a more granular architectural investigation and a greater diversity of benchmark datasets and models. The inclusion of Llama 3 or more targeted models, including an ablation of the GRPO or other models that utilize CoT methodologies could have greatly improved the significance of the paper.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Unlock the Power of Unlabeled Data in Language Driving Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10586v1)
- **Authors**: Chaoqun Wang, Jie Yang, Xiaobin Hong, Ruimao Zhang
- **Abstract**: Recent Vision-based Large Language Models~(VisionLLMs) for autonomous driving have seen rapid advancements. However, such promotion is extremely dependent on large-scale high-quality annotated data, which is costly and labor-intensive. To address this issue, we propose unlocking the value of abundant yet unlabeled data to improve the language-driving model in a semi-supervised learning manner. Specifically, we first introduce a series of template-based prompts to extract scene information, generating questions that create pseudo-answers for the unlabeled data based on a model trained with limited labeled data. Next, we propose a Self-Consistency Refinement method to improve the quality of these pseudo-annotations, which are later used for further training. By utilizing a pre-trained VisionLLM (e.g., InternVL), we build a strong Language Driving Model (LDM) for driving scene question-answering, outperforming previous state-of-the-art methods. Extensive experiments on the DriveLM benchmark show that our approach performs well with just 5% labeled data, achieving competitive performance against models trained with full datasets. In particular, our LDM achieves 44.85% performance with limited labeled data, increasing to 54.27% when using unlabeled data, while models trained with full datasets reach 60.68% on the DriveLM benchmark.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper proposes a semi-supervised learning approach to improve Language Driving Models (LDMs) using unlabeled data. The method first generates pseudo-labels for unlabeled data using a template-based prompt and a model trained on a small amount of labeled data. It then introduces a Self-Consistency Refinement (SCR) method to improve the quality of these pseudo-annotations. SCR uses graph-based hints extracted from the scene to re-ask questions and refine the pseudo-labels based on the consistency between original and re-asked answers.  The refined pseudo-labels are used to further train the LDM. Experiments on the DriveLM benchmark show that the proposed approach performs well with limited labeled data and achieves competitive performance compared to models trained with full datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the specific application of semi-supervised learning to language-driven autonomous driving, particularly the use of graph-based hints within the SCR to refine pseudo-labels.  While semi-supervised learning and pseudo-labeling are established techniques, their combination with scene graph information and consistency checks tailored for the autonomous driving domain constitutes a notable contribution. The idea of using a model's own reasoning to create more accurate data is also a strong point. However, template-based prompt engineering is a very common approach and reduces the novelty somewhat.

* **Significance:** The significance stems from the potential to reduce the reliance on expensive and labor-intensive manual annotation for training LDMs. By effectively utilizing readily available unlabeled data, the proposed approach can make LDMs more accessible and scalable.  The improved performance with limited labeled data has direct implications for the practical deployment of these models. Also, focusing on how to improve *existing* driving datasets is significant.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the challenges of training LDMs with limited labeled data and presents a well-motivated solution.
    * **Sound Methodology:** The proposed SCR method is technically sound and effectively utilizes scene graph information for pseudo-label refinement. The paper uses a credible VisionLLM (InternVL2) and presents a full ablations section to illustrate the effectiveness of each component.
    * **Strong Experimental Results:** The experiments on the DriveLM benchmark demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed approach, with competitive performance achieved with significantly less labeled data. The results are further strengthened with experiments on the validation set.
    * **Well-Written and Organized:** The paper is well-written, clearly explaining the methodology and experimental setup.

* **Weaknesses:**
