### From TOWER to SPIRE: Adding the Speech Modality to a Text-Only LLM
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10620v1)
- **Authors**: Kshitij Ambilduke, Ben Peters, Sonal Sannigrahi, Anil Keshwani, Tsz Kin Lam, Bruno Martins, Marcely Zanon Boito, André F. T. Martins
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have shown remarkable performance and generalization capabilities across multiple languages and tasks, making them very attractive targets for multi-modality integration (e.g., images or speech). In this work, we extend an existing LLM to the speech modality via speech discretization and continued pre-training. In particular, we are interested in multilingual LLMs, such as TOWER, as their pre-training setting allows us to treat discretized speech input as an additional translation language. The resulting open-source model, SPIRE, is able to transcribe and translate English speech input while maintaining TOWER's original performance on translation-related tasks, showcasing that discretized speech input integration as an additional language is feasible during LLM adaptation. We make our code and models available to the community.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SPIRE, a speech-augmented large language model (LLM) built upon the multilingual TOWER model. The authors aim to integrate speech processing capabilities into an existing LLM without sacrificing its original textual performance. This is achieved through speech discretization (using HuBERT-based k-means clustering to generate discrete speech units or DSUs) followed by continued pre-training (CPT) and instruction tuning (IT).  The resulting SPIRE model can perform English speech transcription and translation while maintaining TOWER's translation performance.  The authors present a pipeline for speech integration, comparing the impact of CPT and IT stages, and achieving ASR and ST results competitive with larger, speech-centric models. They emphasize that the code and models are made available for community use.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** While the idea of integrating speech into LLMs isn't entirely new, the paper's approach and focus offer some interesting aspects:
    * **Multilingual Setting:** Building upon a multilingual model (TOWER) to integrate speech introduces complexity.  The idea of treating discretized speech as an additional "language" in the pre-training process is a reasonable approach for a multilingual model, though not groundbreaking.
    * **Preserving Textual Performance:** A key contribution is the focus on maintaining the existing textual capabilities of the LLM while adding speech.  Many speech-LLM integrations prioritize speech tasks, sometimes at the expense of textual performance. This is a well-motivated goal.
    * **Detailed Ablation Studies:** The paper presents comprehensive ablation studies that meticulously investigate the contributions of continued pre-training, instruction tuning, and various datasets.

* **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in:
    * **Demonstrating Feasibility:** It clearly shows that integrating speech as an additional "language" is a feasible and efficient approach during LLM adaptation, particularly for models with a multilingual pre-training setup.
    * **Reproducibility:** The public release of code, models, and datasets is a significant contribution, enabling further research and development.
    * **Benchmarking and Analysis:** The paper provides a useful benchmark of performance in ASR, MT, and ST tasks, allowing comparisons with existing systems and highlighting the strengths and weaknesses of the approach.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates its research question and motivates the approach.
    * **Well-Defined Methodology:** The training process, datasets, and experimental setup are well described.
    * **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The evaluation covers multiple tasks (ASR, MT, ST) with appropriate metrics and baselines.
    * **Thorough Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the importance of different training stages and data components.
    * **Reproducibility:** The availability of code and models is commendable and increases the paper's impact.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Novelty in the Core Technique:** The fundamental idea of using discrete speech units is well-established.  The main novelty comes from the specific application in the multilingual setting and the focus on preserving textual performance.
    * **Incremental Improvement:**  The paper demonstrates competent performance, but doesn't achieve breakthrough results.  The ASR performance, while competitive, doesn't surpass the best speech-centric models by a significant margin.
    * **Somewhat Task-Specific:** The integration with TOWER and focus on speech->English translation limits the generality of the approach.

* **Potential Influence:**
    * The paper's approach could influence future research on integrating speech into multilingual LLMs, especially where maintaining existing textual capabilities is critical.
    * The reproducible pipeline will facilitate further experiments and improvements in this area.

**Score:** 6

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a solid piece of work, but lacks truly transformative novelty. While the integration of speech into a multilingual LLM is interesting, it relies on existing techniques for speech discretization. The main strength is the demonstrated ability to preserve textual performance while adding speech, backed by thorough experiments and a reproducible pipeline. The benefits of text data integration can be more rigorously explored. However, the incremental nature of the improvements and the reliance on a particular architecture (TOWER) limit its potential to revolutionize the field.  The score reflects the paper's sound methodology, well-defined goal, and contribution of a reproducible resource to the research community, while acknowledging the limited novelty and incremental advancements.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Transformers without Normalization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10622v1)
- **Authors**: Jiachen Zhu, Xinlei Chen, Kaiming He, Yann LeCun, Zhuang Liu
- **Abstract**: Normalization layers are ubiquitous in modern neural networks and have long been considered essential. This work demonstrates that Transformers without normalization can achieve the same or better performance using a remarkably simple technique. We introduce Dynamic Tanh (DyT), an element-wise operation $DyT($x$) = \tanh(\alpha $x$)$, as a drop-in replacement for normalization layers in Transformers. DyT is inspired by the observation that layer normalization in Transformers often produces tanh-like, $S$-shaped input-output mappings. By incorporating DyT, Transformers without normalization can match or exceed the performance of their normalized counterparts, mostly without hyperparameter tuning. We validate the effectiveness of Transformers with DyT across diverse settings, ranging from recognition to generation, supervised to self-supervised learning, and computer vision to language models. These findings challenge the conventional understanding that normalization layers are indispensable in modern neural networks, and offer new insights into their role in deep networks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper challenges the conventional wisdom that normalization layers are indispensable in modern neural networks, particularly Transformers. It introduces a simple, element-wise operation called Dynamic Tanh (DyT), defined as DyT(x) = tanh(ax), where 'a' is a learnable parameter. DyT is proposed as a drop-in replacement for normalization layers like LayerNorm (LN) and RMSNorm. The authors hypothesize that LN layers often produce tanh-like input-output mappings and DyT can emulate this behavior by learning an appropriate scaling factor 'a' and squashing extreme values via the tanh function, without computing activation statistics.  They demonstrate that Transformers with DyT can achieve comparable or better performance than their normalized counterparts across various tasks, including image recognition, self-supervised learning, language modeling, and image generation.  They also present analysis showing how the learnable parameter 'a' dynamically adjusts to the input data statistics during training and improves both training and inference speed.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The central idea of replacing normalization with a learnable scaled tanh activation is reasonably novel. While the individual components (tanh, learnable scaling) are not new, their combination as a drop-in replacement for normalization layers and the accompanying analysis of LN behavior do present a fresh perspective. The observation that LN layers create tanh-like mappings is insightful and provides a strong justification for the DyT approach.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in potentially simplifying network architectures and offering an alternative perspective on the role of normalization. If the findings hold up in further research and wider adoption, it could lead to faster and more efficient training of Transformers. The ablation studies and analysis (e.g., the behavior of 'a' during training) are crucial for understanding why DyT works and contribute to the theoretical understanding of normalization. The reduced computation time (lower latency in the BF16 setting) and reduction in parameters is an excellent contribution.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper presents convincing empirical evidence across diverse tasks and architectures, showcasing DyT's ability to match or exceed LN's performance, which strengthens its credibility.
    *   **Clear Motivation and Justification:** The observation about tanh-like mappings is well-motivated and supported by visualizations, providing a clear rationale for the DyT design.
    *   **Simplicity:** The DyT approach is remarkably simple, making it easy to implement and integrate into existing architectures.
    *   **Detailed Ablation Studies:** The ablations effectively demonstrate the importance of both the tanh function and the learnable parameter 'a' in DyT.
    *   **Analysis of 'a' Behavior:** Understanding how 'a' dynamically adjusts during training and its correlation with input activation statistics provides insights into DyT's effectiveness.
    *   **Reduced computation time** DyT reduces the computation time for a model, a significant advantage.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Theoretical Explanation:** While the paper provides some analysis, a deeper theoretical understanding of why DyT works as a normalization alternative is lacking. The connection between DyT and gradient flow or loss landscape smoothing needs further investigation. It struggles to replace BN in ResNets.
    *   **LLM specific scalar is somewhat ad-hoc:** Introducing a scalar after the embedding layer in LLMs could be better motivated. This appears more like an engineering fix rather than a principle architectural change. The need to tune the initialization of this scalar is also a weakness.
    *   **Dependency on LLM model width:** The dependency of α initialization with LLM width is an issue. This makes it harder to optimize.
    *  **The paper could have been more critical on why replacing BN fails:**

*   **Potential Impact:** If DyT proves robust and widely applicable, it could significantly impact the field by:

    *   Simplifying Transformer architectures.
    *   Potentially reducing training and inference costs.
    *   Offering a new perspective on the role of normalization and activation functions.

**Overall:** The paper offers a valuable contribution by challenging existing assumptions and presenting a viable alternative to normalization layers in Transformers. The empirical results are strong, and the analysis provides some insights into DyT's behavior. The limited theoretical understanding and some LLM quirks are a drawback. While it may not completely revolutionize the field, it is a worthwhile contribution.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### NIL: No-data Imitation Learning by Leveraging Pre-trained Video Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10626v1)
- **Authors**: Mert Albaba, Chenhao Li, Markos Diomataris, Omid Taheri, Andreas Krause, Michael Black
- **Abstract**: Acquiring physically plausible motor skills across diverse and unconventional morphologies-including humanoid robots, quadrupeds, and animals-is essential for advancing character simulation and robotics. Traditional methods, such as reinforcement learning (RL) are task- and body-specific, require extensive reward function engineering, and do not generalize well. Imitation learning offers an alternative but relies heavily on high-quality expert demonstrations, which are difficult to obtain for non-human morphologies. Video diffusion models, on the other hand, are capable of generating realistic videos of various morphologies, from humans to ants. Leveraging this capability, we propose a data-independent approach for skill acquisition that learns 3D motor skills from 2D-generated videos, with generalization capability to unconventional and non-human forms. Specifically, we guide the imitation learning process by leveraging vision transformers for video-based comparisons by calculating pair-wise distance between video embeddings. Along with video-encoding distance, we also use a computed similarity between segmented video frames as a guidance reward. We validate our method on locomotion tasks involving unique body configurations. In humanoid robot locomotion tasks, we demonstrate that 'No-data Imitation Learning' (NIL) outperforms baselines trained on 3D motion-capture data. Our results highlight the potential of leveraging generative video models for physically plausible skill learning with diverse morphologies, effectively replacing data collection with data generation for imitation learning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "NIL: No-data Imitation Learning by Leveraging Pre-trained Video Diffusion Models" introduces a novel approach to imitation learning (IL) called No-data Imitation Learning (NIL). NIL bypasses the need for high-quality expert demonstrations by leveraging pre-trained video diffusion models to generate reference videos. A reinforcement learning agent is then trained in a physical simulator to mimic the generated video using a reward function based on video encoding similarity, image segmentation mask IoU, and regularization terms. The authors demonstrate that NIL can effectively learn locomotion skills for diverse robot morphologies without relying on any real-world or simulated expert data, and that it can even outperform baselines trained on motion-capture data.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using video diffusion models to generate training data for imitation learning is novel and promising. While related works exist that use generated data, NIL distinguishes itself by *completely* eliminating the need for curated expert data, relying solely on a generative model and visual similarity. The specific combination of video diffusion models, vision transformers for encoding, and segmentation-based IoU for reward shaping also seems to be a unique contribution. The rigorous experimentation comparing the impact of each component of the reward is a plus.

*   **Significance:**  The significance lies in the potential to democratize robot learning and character animation. Acquiring high-quality motion capture data for various robot morphologies is often a major bottleneck. If NIL proves to be robust and scalable, it could significantly reduce the cost and effort associated with training robots, leading to faster development cycles and greater accessibility. Furthermore, NIL opens up exciting possibilities for training agents to perform tasks for which demonstrations are inherently difficult or impossible to obtain. However, the method is only validated in a relatively simple domain (locomotion), and it is not clear how well it can be scaled to more complex tasks such as manipulation. The choice of a reward that focuses on similarity can have negative consequences since it can lead to exploitation of errors by the generative model to find shortcuts.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Addresses a key problem in robot learning.
    *   Novel and potentially transformative approach.
    *   Well-defined method with clear implementation details.
    *   Strong experimental results on locomotion tasks.
    *   Comprehensive ablation studies.
    *   Demonstration on various robot morphologies.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Limited to relatively simple tasks (locomotion). The generalizability to more complex manipulation tasks is not well addressed.
    *   Performance heavily reliant on the quality of the video diffusion model, which may still struggle with physically plausible motions, particularly in uncommon configurations or dynamic scenarios. The results indicate instability (varying performance based on different Kling versions).
    *   The evaluation is primarily conducted in simulation, and it is not clear how well the learned policies will transfer to the real world.
    *   The dependence on the similarity in the video can introduce bias because the system will exploit the generative model to learn tasks.
    *   Although the method does not require curated data, it heavily depends on the availability of video diffusion models which requires large computational resources to be trained.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to be highly influential if it can be extended to more complex tasks and demonstrates robustness in real-world settings. It could inspire new research directions in imitation learning, generative modeling, and robotics. The paper's success also depends on the continued advancement and accessibility of high-quality video diffusion models.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and promising approach to imitation learning that addresses a significant challenge in the field. The experimental results are compelling, and the ablation studies provide valuable insights. However, the limitations regarding task complexity, dependence on generative model quality, and lack of real-world validation temper the overall assessment. The score reflects the paper's significant potential but acknowledges the need for further research to fully realize its impact. The novelty is very good but not groundbreaking, and the contribution is significant yet domain-constrained at the moment. The validation in a simple environment and task limits the scope of the paper. Future works can focus on addressing such limitations for enhancing the score.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### SciVerse: Unveiling the Knowledge Comprehension and Visual Reasoning of LMMs on Multi-modal Scientific Problems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10627v1)
- **Authors**: Ziyu Guo, Ray Zhang, Hao Chen, Jialin Gao, Dongzhi Jiang, Jiaze Wang, Pheng-Ann Heng
- **Abstract**: The rapid advancement of Large Multi-modal Models (LMMs) has enabled their application in scientific problem-solving, yet their fine-grained capabilities remain under-explored. In this paper, we introduce SciVerse, a multi-modal scientific evaluation benchmark to thoroughly assess LMMs across 5,735 test instances in five distinct versions. We aim to investigate three key dimensions of LMMs: scientific knowledge comprehension, multi-modal content interpretation, and Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning. To unveil whether LMMs possess sufficient scientific expertise, we first transform each problem into three versions containing different levels of knowledge required for solving, i.e., Knowledge-free, -lite, and -rich. Then, to explore how LMMs interpret multi-modal scientific content, we annotate another two versions, i.e., Vision-rich and -only, marking more question information from texts to diagrams. Comparing the results of different versions, SciVerse systematically examines the professional knowledge stock and visual perception skills of LMMs in scientific domains. In addition, to rigorously assess CoT reasoning, we propose a new scientific CoT evaluation strategy, conducting a step-wise assessment on knowledge and logical errors in model outputs. Our extensive evaluation of different LMMs on SciVerse reveals critical limitations in their scientific proficiency and provides new insights into future developments. Project page: https://sciverse-cuhk.github.io
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SCIVERSE, a new multi-modal scientific evaluation benchmark designed to assess the knowledge comprehension and visual reasoning abilities of Large Multi-modal Models (LMMs) on scientific problems. SCIVERSE comprises 5,735 test instances across five distinct versions of 1,147 problems spanning physics, chemistry, and biology. The benchmark is designed to investigate three key dimensions of LMMs: scientific knowledge comprehension (through Knowledge-free, -lite, and -rich versions), multi-modal content interpretation (Vision-rich and -only versions), and Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning (evaluated using a novel step-wise assessment strategy). The paper presents evaluation results for a variety of open-source and closed-source LMMs, highlighting critical limitations in their scientific proficiency, particularly in visual perception and knowledge integration. The authors also introduce a scientific CoT evaluation strategy, focusing on step-wise errors in knowledge review and reasoning deduction, which aims for a more comprehensive analysis.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive Benchmark:** SCIVERSE provides a significantly more thorough evaluation of LMMs in scientific contexts compared to existing benchmarks. The inclusion of multiple versions of each problem allows for fine-grained analysis of specific limitations, such as knowledge deficiencies and visual interpretation challenges.
*   **Targeted Skill Assessment:** The focus on scientific knowledge comprehension, multi-modal content interpretation, and CoT reasoning are relevant and necessary for evaluating LMMs in scientific applications.
*   **Novel CoT Evaluation:** The step-wise evaluation of CoT reasoning, assessing both knowledge and logical errors, is a strong contribution. This provides a more nuanced understanding of LMM reasoning capabilities beyond simply assessing final answer accuracy.
*   **Diverse Model Evaluation:** The evaluation of both open-source and closed-source LMMs provides a comprehensive overview of the current state-of-the-art.
*   **Clear Problem Formulation:** The paper clearly articulates the research questions and the design choices behind SCIVERSE, making it easy to understand the benchmark's purpose and scope.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Dataset Curation Effort:** While meticulous, manually creating and annotating 5,735 test instances is a significant limitation. Scalability and the potential for bias in the annotation process are concerns. The paper mentions PhD-level science experts, but it needs to elaborate the actual process they performed.
*   **Complexity of Evaluation:**  The CoT evaluation relies on GPT-40, which introduces another layer of complexity and dependency. The correctness scores (Sci-CoTK and Sci-CoTL) still are not enough to capture all the mistakes of intermediate steps.
*   **Limited Task Diversity:**  The benchmark focuses on multiple-choice questions. While common, this format might not fully capture the problem-solving skills required in real-world scientific research, which often involves open-ended reasoning, hypothesis generation, and experimental design.
*   **Potential Realism Concerns:** The Vision-only problems, while aiming for realism, still rely on clear diagrams with text embedded. Real-world visual data (e.g., microscope images, complex experimental setups) presents significantly greater challenges.
*   **Lack of ablation study:** The impact of the new evaluation strategy has not been shown. It's hard to compare with previous binary metrics without such a comparison.

**Novelty and Significance:**

SCIVERSE represents a significant advancement in evaluating LMMs for scientific applications. The detailed analysis of knowledge comprehension, multi-modal integration, and CoT reasoning fills a critical gap in the existing literature. The benchmark is well-designed and addresses important limitations of previous approaches. The results demonstrate the existing shortcomings of LMMs in scientific domains, which is an important contribution. The proposed framework and CoT evaluation method offer researchers a solid basis for investigating and improving the scientific proficiency of LMMs.

**Potential Influence:**

SCIVERSE has the potential to significantly influence the development and evaluation of LMMs for scientific tasks. The benchmark can be used to:

*   Guide the development of more scientifically proficient LMMs.
*   Identify specific areas where LMMs need improvement (e.g., visual reasoning, knowledge integration).
*   Compare the performance of different LMM architectures.
*   Track progress in the field over time.

However, its reliance on careful manual curation is a limiting factor. Future research should focus on creating more scalable and automated approaches for generating and evaluating scientific benchmarks.

**Overall:**

SCIVERSE is a well-designed and important contribution to the field of multi-modal scientific reasoning. While there are some limitations, the strengths of the benchmark outweigh the weaknesses. This framework fills important gaps in existing literature, providing a crucial step in establishing a robust approach for scientific-level benchmarks for LMMs.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Uncertainty in Action: Confidence Elicitation in Embodied Agents
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10628v1)
- **Authors**: Tianjiao Yu, Vedant Shah, Muntasir Wahed, Kiet A. Nguyen, Adheesh Juvekar, Tal August, Ismini Lourentzou
- **Abstract**: Expressing confidence is challenging for embodied agents navigating dynamic multimodal environments, where uncertainty arises from both perception and decision-making processes. We present the first work investigating embodied confidence elicitation in open-ended multimodal environments. We introduce Elicitation Policies, which structure confidence assessment across inductive, deductive, and abductive reasoning, along with Execution Policies, which enhance confidence calibration through scenario reinterpretation, action sampling, and hypothetical reasoning. Evaluating agents in calibration and failure prediction tasks within the Minecraft environment, we show that structured reasoning approaches, such as Chain-of-Thoughts, improve confidence calibration. However, our findings also reveal persistent challenges in distinguishing uncertainty, particularly under abductive settings, underscoring the need for more sophisticated embodied confidence elicitation methods.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel framework for embodied confidence elicitation, addressing the challenges of uncertainty assessment in open-ended, multimodal environments like Minecraft. The authors propose "Elicitation Policies" (inductive, deductive, abductive reasoning) and "Execution Policies" (scenario reinterpretation, action sampling, hypothetical reasoning) to structure confidence assessment and improve calibration. Experiments using LLM/VLM-powered agents within Minecraft demonstrate that structured reasoning approaches (Chain-of-Thoughts, Plan & Solve) enhance confidence calibration but also highlight persistent challenges, especially in abductive settings. The results underscore the need for more sophisticated methods to distinguish and manage uncertainty in embodied AI.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents the *first* systematic investigation of embodied confidence elicitation in open-ended multimodal environments. This constitutes a significant contribution as previous work largely focused on unimodal settings or lacked structured elicitation mechanisms specifically designed for embodied AI. The introduction of Elicitation and Execution Policies provides a novel way to structure the confidence assessment process.

*   **Significance:**  The paper addresses a crucial challenge in the development of reliable and trustworthy embodied AI agents.  Accurate confidence elicitation is vital for risk assessment, error mitigation, and decision-making in real-world applications. The study's insights into the effectiveness and limitations of different elicitation strategies contribute to a better understanding of how to build more robust and transparent AI systems that operate in complex and uncertain environments.  The work's evaluation within the Minecraft environment provides a concrete testbed and benchmark for future research.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear problem definition:** The paper clearly articulates the challenges of confidence elicitation in embodied AI, highlighting the importance of multimodal understanding, granularity of confidence estimation, and interactive dependencies.
    *   **Well-defined framework:** The proposed Elicitation and Execution Policies offer a structured and principled approach to confidence assessment.
    *   **Comprehensive evaluation:** The experimental setup is well-designed, utilizing established Minecraft benchmarks and comparing the performance of different LLM/VLM agents under various elicitation strategies. The use of multiple evaluation metrics (ECE, AUROC, PR-P, PR-N) provides a thorough assessment of calibration and failure prediction.
    *   **Valuable insights:** The paper identifies effective methods for improving confidence calibration (CoT, P&S), reveals persistent challenges (abductive reasoning), and underscores the importance of model-specific tuning.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited model scope:** While the paper evaluates several models (GPT-4V, MineLLM, STEVE), the study is limited to a relatively small set. Expanding the model selection would provide a more generalizable assessment of the proposed framework.
    *   **Task dependence:** The findings are specific to the Minecraft environment. While Minecraft is a useful benchmark, it is important to consider the extent to which the results generalize to other embodied AI domains.
    *   **Explainability:** The paper shows quantitative improvements in calibration and failure prediction, further research is needed to provide deeper qualitative insights into why certain elicitation strategies are more effective than others. Further work should focus on providing richer explanations of the reasoning behind the generated confidence scores.

*   **Potential Influence:** This work is likely to stimulate further research in embodied confidence elicitation. The proposed framework can be used as a foundation for developing more sophisticated elicitation strategies and benchmarking their performance. The paper's findings will guide the design of more reliable and transparent embodied AI systems that can effectively communicate their uncertainty and adapt to complex environments.

*   **Rigorous rationale:**  While the paper presents a solid framework and valuable insights, the limitations on the model diversity and dependence on the minecraft environment suggest that the field is early in understanding how to approach robust confidence elicitation. The core contribution lies in recognizing the importance of incorporating uncertainty into a complex field of research but there's still significant work needed to broaden the applicability and enhance the robustness of findings.

**Score: 7**

The paper makes a significant contribution by being the first to systematically explore embodied confidence elicitation in open-ended, multimodal environments. The proposed framework and experimental results provide valuable insights and a solid foundation for future research. However, the limited model scope and task dependence somewhat constrain the generalizability of the findings. A higher score would be warranted with a broader range of models tested and a more extensive discussion of the limitations.
- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### UniGoal: Towards Universal Zero-shot Goal-oriented Navigation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10630v1)
- **Authors**: Hang Yin, Xiuwei Xu, Lingqing Zhao, Ziwei Wang, Jie Zhou, Jiwen Lu
- **Abstract**: In this paper, we propose a general framework for universal zero-shot goal-oriented navigation. Existing zero-shot methods build inference framework upon large language models (LLM) for specific tasks, which differs a lot in overall pipeline and fails to generalize across different types of goal. Towards the aim of universal zero-shot navigation, we propose a uniform graph representation to unify different goals, including object category, instance image and text description. We also convert the observation of agent into an online maintained scene graph. With this consistent scene and goal representation, we preserve most structural information compared with pure text and are able to leverage LLM for explicit graph-based reasoning. Specifically, we conduct graph matching between the scene graph and goal graph at each time instant and propose different strategies to generate long-term goal of exploration according to different matching states. The agent first iteratively searches subgraph of goal when zero-matched. With partial matching, the agent then utilizes coordinate projection and anchor pair alignment to infer the goal location. Finally scene graph correction and goal verification are applied for perfect matching. We also present a blacklist mechanism to enable robust switch between stages. Extensive experiments on several benchmarks show that our UniGoal achieves state-of-the-art zero-shot performance on three studied navigation tasks with a single model, even outperforming task-specific zero-shot methods and supervised universal methods.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "UniGoal: Towards Universal Zero-shot Goal-oriented Navigation":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces UniGoal, a novel framework designed for universal zero-shot goal-oriented navigation. Unlike existing zero-shot methods that are tailored to specific tasks and struggle to generalize across different goal types, UniGoal employs a uniform graph representation to unify diverse goals like object category, instance image, and text description. It also maintains an online scene graph of the agent's observations. Leveraging Large Language Models (LLMs) for explicit graph-based reasoning, UniGoal performs graph matching between scene and goal graphs, dynamically adjusting its exploration strategy based on the matching state.  The system utilizes a multi-stage exploration policy involving iterative subgraph searching, coordinate projection, anchor pair alignment, scene graph correction, and goal verification, all while implementing a blacklist mechanism to avoid repeated exploration of unsuccessful regions. The authors demonstrate state-of-the-art zero-shot performance across several benchmarks, even outperforming task-specific zero-shot and supervised universal methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates a significant degree of novelty through the development of a unified graph representation scheme that seamlessly handles different goal types.  The multi-stage exploration policy, combined with the blacklist mechanism, also presents a novel approach to achieving efficient navigation in zero-shot settings.  The integration of LLMs for graph reasoning in this context is also a strong point. However, using LLMs for scene understanding and planning is not new. The novelty primarily lies in *how* these are combined.

*   **Significance:** The UniGoal framework's capacity to perform effectively across diverse goal types, without requiring any training or finetuning, presents a substantial advancement. This has significant implications for real-world robotics applications where agent versatility and adaptability are paramount.  The performance improvements over existing zero-shot and supervised methods further bolster the paper's significance. The ablations clearly illustrate the value of each stage, enhancing the impact.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Unified Representation:** The graph-based representation is a key strength, enabling a single framework to handle various goal types.
    *   **Multi-Stage Policy:** The adaptive exploration policy is well-designed, making efficient use of LLM-based reasoning.
    *   **Strong Results:** State-of-the-art performance across multiple benchmarks demonstrates the effectiveness of the proposed approach.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** Thorough ablation studies provide insights into the importance of each component.
    *   **Handling of Visual and Language Goals:** Capable of handling vision-related (Image Goal) and language-related (Text Goal) navigation, which expands potential real-world applications.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on LLMs/VLMs:** Like many modern approaches, the performance is heavily dependent on the capabilities of underlying LLMs and VLMs. This can be a limitation as these models are resource-intensive and might have biases. While the reliance on pre-trained models is acknowledged, it's a significant architectural dependency.
    *   **Limited Real-World Deployment:** The paper lacks extensive real-world deployment results. While they mention real-world deployment briefly, more detail would enhance the significance further.
    *   **Complexity:**  The system's architecture is somewhat complex, involving multiple stages and components.  While well-explained, there's a potential concern regarding computational efficiency.

*   **Impact:**  UniGoal has the potential to influence future research in zero-shot navigation, particularly in developing more versatile and adaptable agents. The graph-based representation and multi-stage exploration policy could inspire new approaches to navigation in complex environments. The real-world demonstration will be crucial for wider adoption.

**Justification for Score:**

While the UniGoal framework represents a significant improvement in zero-shot goal-oriented navigation, its dependency on powerful LLMs and the limited real-world results prevent it from achieving an exceptional score. The novelty is good, and the performance gains are impressive, but the fundamental reliance on pre-trained models and lack of strong real-world validation hold it back. The method’s complexity and computational resource requirements also slightly temper its immediate practical impact.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### HybridVLA: Collaborative Diffusion and Autoregression in a Unified Vision-Language-Action Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10631v1)
- **Authors**: Jiaming Liu, Hao Chen, Pengju An, Zhuoyang Liu, Renrui Zhang, Chenyang Gu, Xiaoqi Li, Ziyu Guo, Sixiang Chen, Mengzhen Liu, Chengkai Hou, Mengdi Zhao, KC alex Zhou, Pheng-Ann Heng, Shanghang Zhang
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in vision-language models (VLMs) for common-sense reasoning have led to the development of vision-language-action (VLA) models, enabling robots to perform generalized manipulation. Although existing autoregressive VLA methods leverage large-scale pretrained knowledge, they disrupt the continuity of actions. Meanwhile, some VLA methods incorporate an additional diffusion head to predict continuous actions, relying solely on VLM-extracted features, which limits their reasoning capabilities. In this paper, we introduce HybridVLA, a unified framework that seamlessly integrates the strengths of both autoregressive and diffusion policies within a single large language model, rather than simply connecting them. To bridge the generation gap, a collaborative training recipe is proposed that injects the diffusion modeling directly into the next-token prediction. With this recipe, we find that these two forms of action prediction not only reinforce each other but also exhibit varying performance across different tasks. Therefore, we design a collaborative action ensemble mechanism that adaptively fuses these two predictions, leading to more robust control. In experiments, HybridVLA outperforms previous state-of-the-art VLA methods across various simulation and real-world tasks, including both single-arm and dual-arm robots, while demonstrating stable manipulation in previously unseen configurations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces HybridVLA, a new vision-language-action (VLA) model that unifies diffusion and autoregressive action prediction within a single large language model (LLM). Unlike existing approaches that either discretize actions for autoregressive prediction or append a separate diffusion head to the VLM, HybridVLA integrates diffusion modeling directly into the next-token prediction process of the LLM.  The authors propose a collaborative training recipe to bridge the gap between the two action generation methods and use a collaborative action ensemble mechanism to adaptively fuse predictions, leading to robust control.  Experiments show that HybridVLA outperforms state-of-the-art VLA models on simulation and real-world tasks, demonstrating stable manipulation and generalization to unseen configurations. The paper also proposes an optimized version (HybridVLA-dif) for faster inference by relying solely on diffusion-based actions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The core novelty of this paper lies in the *unified integration* of diffusion and autoregressive action prediction within a *single* LLM. While previous works have explored diffusion policies or autoregressive VLAs, the seamless combination and collaborative training approach are novel. The collaborative training recipe that injects diffusion modeling directly into the next-token prediction is also a significant contribution. The design of marker tokens to ensure consistency between the two generation methods is a technically sound approach.

**Significance:** The significance stems from addressing the limitations of both existing paradigms. Autoregressive methods, while leveraging LLM knowledge, often suffer from disrupted action continuity. Diffusion methods, while providing continuous actions, might lack reasoning capabilities if decoupled from the LLM. HybridVLA aims to overcome these limitations, leading to improved performance and generalization. The experiments are thorough, covering both simulation and real-world scenarios, and demonstrate substantial improvements over existing methods. The focus on improving inference speed via HybridVLA-dif is also a practical contribution.

**Strengths:**

*   **Unified Architecture:**  A single LLM architecture is used for both action generation schemes.
*   **Collaborative Training:**  The proposed training approach is sound and shows clear benefits.
*   **Action Ensemble:** The adaptive fusion mechanism is a clever approach to leverage the strengths of both methods.
*   **Extensive Experiments:**  Comprehensive evaluation in simulation and real-world settings provides strong evidence for the effectiveness of the approach.
*   **Generalization Results:** The paper demonstrates generalization to unseen objects, backgrounds, and lighting, which is a critical aspect of real-world applicability.
*   **Optimized inference:** The focus on creating the optimized inference model which uses the diffusion-based action to perform the same with 9.4HZ.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Inference Time:** The inference time of HybridVLA is still a limitation, as noted by the authors. While HybridVLA-dif addresses this, it would be more impactful if the full HybridVLA could achieve competitive inference speeds. This may indicate a sub-optimal action ensemble method, and it would be interesting to evaluate the computational overhead required to produce the actions.
*   **Complexity:** The HybridVLA framework is relatively complex with many components, collaborative training, KV cache. Further simplification of the training framework may reduce some technical barriers.
*  **Ablation studies:** The ablation studies do a great job of evaluating each loss, however, additional studies analyzing the effectiveness of the individual components within the ensemble may be impactful to further understand the mechanism by which they lead to higher performance.

**Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of VLA modeling. The unified framework and collaborative training approach could become a standard practice. The insights into the strengths and weaknesses of autoregressive and diffusion policies can guide future research. If the inference speed of the full HybridVLA can be further improved, it could become a highly practical solution for real-world robot manipulation.

**Score: 8**

**Justification:**  The paper presents a technically sound and novel approach to VLA modeling, addresses a key limitation of existing methods, and demonstrates significant improvements in performance and generalization. While inference speed remains a limitation, the optimized version for faster inference indicates an understanding of the model complexity. The comprehensive experimental validation and the potential impact on the field support the score. The combination of the diffusion and the LLM with the novel combination of both within a single architecture is quite novel, lending itself to the high score. A score of 9 or 10 would require an even more revolutionary leap, likely including improvements on the high compute resource requirements.
- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Kolmogorov-Arnold Attention: Is Learnable Attention Better For Vision Transformers?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10632v1)
- **Authors**: Subhajit Maity, Killian Hitsman, Xin Li, Aritra Dutta
- **Abstract**: Kolmogorov-Arnold networks (KANs) are a remarkable innovation consisting of learnable activation functions with the potential to capture more complex relationships from data. Although KANs are useful in finding symbolic representations and continual learning of one-dimensional functions, their effectiveness in diverse machine learning (ML) tasks, such as vision, remains questionable. Presently, KANs are deployed by replacing multilayer perceptrons (MLPs) in deep network architectures, including advanced architectures such as vision Transformers (ViTs). In this paper, we are the first to design a general learnable Kolmogorov-Arnold Attention (KArAt) for vanilla ViTs that can operate on any choice of basis. However, the computing and memory costs of training them motivated us to propose a more modular version, and we designed particular learnable attention, called Fourier-KArAt. Fourier-KArAt and its variants either outperform their ViT counterparts or show comparable performance on CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, and ImageNet-1K datasets. We dissect these architectures' performance and generalization capacity by analyzing their loss landscapes, weight distributions, optimizer path, attention visualization, and spectral behavior, and contrast them with vanilla ViTs. The goal of this paper is not to produce parameter- and compute-efficient attention, but to encourage the community to explore KANs in conjunction with more advanced architectures that require a careful understanding of learnable activations. Our open-source code and implementation details are available on: https://subhajitmaity.me/KArAt
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the potential of replacing the standard softmax self-attention mechanism in Vision Transformers (ViTs) with a learnable Kolmogorov-Arnold Attention (KArAt) module.  They design a general KArAt module and, to address computational challenges, propose a more modular version called Fourier-KArAt. The performance of Fourier-KArAt and its variants are benchmarked against vanilla ViTs on CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, and ImageNet-1K. The authors analyze the performance by examining loss landscapes, weight distributions, optimizer paths, attention visualizations, and spectral behavior to understand the differences between KArAt and vanilla ViTs. They conclude that simply replacing softmax with KANs doesn't guarantee better performance and a careful design of the KAN architecture is critical. The paper also highlights challenges in scaling learnable attention due to computational costs and suggests further research is needed to explore the potential of KANs in more advanced architectures.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using KANs for attention is not entirely novel, as referenced, the KAT paper by Yang & Wang, 2025, also uses KANs in Vision Transformers. However, the current paper focuses on designing a general and modular learnable attention mechanism, particularly using Fourier basis functions, which appears to be a unique contribution. The exploration of different configurations (blockwise vs. universal) and the analysis of spectral behavior, loss landscapes, and weight distributions are also valuable. However, the KAT paper also provides a refined group-KAN strategy, which the current work does not substantially improve upon.

*   **Significance:** The significance is somewhat limited. While the paper provides a thorough analysis and benchmark of KArAt, it doesn't demonstrate a substantial improvement in performance compared to vanilla ViTs, especially on larger datasets like ImageNet-1K.  The paper's main value lies in its analysis and insights into why simply replacing softmax with KANs isn't sufficient and in identifying the challenges associated with learnable attention. The findings suggest further research directions, but the immediate impact is not high. Furthermore, the computation complexity of implementing and training these modules is a notable downside.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Thorough experimental evaluation across multiple datasets and ViT architectures.
    *   Detailed analysis of different aspects of the models (loss landscapes, weight distributions, attention visualization, spectral analysis).
    *   Clear presentation of the challenges associated with learnable attention and KANs in ViTs.
    *   Good literature review and comparison with existing works.
    * Addresses a potentially important question for further architecture development.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Limited improvement in performance compared to vanilla ViTs. In some cases, the KArAt variants perform worse.
    *   High computational cost makes it difficult to scale and experiment with larger models.
    *   The paper acknowledges that KAT and previous approaches explored KANs, thus limiting novelty.
    *   The conclusions primarily point out difficulties and directions for future work rather than presenting a significant breakthrough.
    * The ablation experiments while detailed are not always conclusive in highlighting one setting over another for KAN parameters.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence future research by:
    *   Highlighting the importance of careful KAN design and the limitations of a simple "drop-in replacement" approach.
    *   Encouraging exploration of more efficient implementations of learnable attention.
    *   Inspiring new architectures that leverage KANs in more sophisticated ways.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a reasonably thorough empirical investigation into the impact of learnable attention through KANs in ViTs. However, the limited performance gains, and the high computational overhead, hinder its significance. It also lacks enough novelty to warrant a high score. This leads to an overall score of **4/10**. It is a solid piece of work in terms of analysis and experimental setup, but the lack of tangible improvements and significant novelty limits its overall impact.
Score: 4

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 4/10

### Distilling Diversity and Control in Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10637v1)
- **Authors**: Rohit Gandikota, David Bau
- **Abstract**: Distilled diffusion models suffer from a critical limitation: reduced sample diversity compared to their base counterparts. In this work, we uncover that despite this diversity loss, distilled models retain the fundamental concept representations of base models. We demonstrate control distillation - where control mechanisms like Concept Sliders and LoRAs trained on base models can be seamlessly transferred to distilled models and vice-versa, effectively distilling control without any retraining. This preservation of representational structure prompted our investigation into the mechanisms of diversity collapse during distillation. To understand how distillation affects diversity, we introduce Diffusion Target (DT) Visualization, an analysis and debugging tool that reveals how models predict final outputs at intermediate steps. Through DT-Visualization, we identify generation artifacts, inconsistencies, and demonstrate that initial diffusion timesteps disproportionately determine output diversity, while later steps primarily refine details. Based on these insights, we introduce diversity distillation - a hybrid inference approach that strategically employs the base model for only the first critical timestep before transitioning to the efficient distilled model. Our experiments demonstrate that this simple modification not only restores the diversity capabilities from base to distilled models but surprisingly exceeds it, while maintaining nearly the computational efficiency of distilled inference, all without requiring additional training or model modifications. Our code and data are available at https://distillation.baulab.info
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Distilling Diversity and Control in Diffusion Models":

**Summary:**

This paper addresses the problem of reduced diversity in distilled diffusion models, which are designed for faster image generation but sacrifice the diversity of outputs compared to their slower base model counterparts. The authors make several key contributions. First, they demonstrate that distilled models retain the *concept representations* of base models, allowing control mechanisms (like concept sliders and LoRAs) trained on base models to be directly applied to distilled versions (and vice-versa) without retraining. Second, they introduce a novel analysis technique called "Diffusion Target Visualization" (DT-Visualization) which reveals that early timesteps in the diffusion process disproportionately influence the structural composition and diversity of the generated images, while later timesteps primarily refine details. Finally, based on these insights, they propose a "diversity distillation" approach: a hybrid inference method that utilizes the base model for the initial diversity-critical timestep and then switches to the efficient distilled model for the remaining steps. This approach restores (and sometimes exceeds) the diversity of the base model, while maintaining the speed of distilled inference.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper makes several interesting contributions that advance the understanding of diffusion model distillation.

*   **Novelty:** The DT-Visualization technique is a significant novel contribution.  It provides a valuable debugging and analytical tool for understanding the inner workings of diffusion models, especially how information is encoded at different timesteps. The observation that early timesteps are crucial for diversity is non-obvious and provides a solid foundation for their proposed solution. The hybrid inference approach, while conceptually simple, is a direct and effective application of their analysis.  The demonstration of "control distillation" is also a valuable finding, highlighting the representational stability during distillation.

*   **Significance:** The practical impact of addressing the diversity loss in distilled models is considerable. Fast and diverse image generation is highly desirable for deployment scenarios. The diversity distillation approach offers a practical way to achieve this without significant additional training. The control distillation result opens doors to leveraging existing control mechanisms on more efficient models. The DT-Visualization technique could inspire further analysis and improvements in both base and distilled diffusion models. The results suggesting the first timestep is critical is a significant insight, as it also opens the door to exploring ways to improve diversity without needing to rely on a much slower base model.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper is well-written and clearly articulates its findings.
    *   The DT-Visualization technique is thoroughly explained and demonstrated.
    *   The experimental results are convincing and support the claims made.
    *   The hybrid inference approach is simple to implement and yields significant improvements.
    *   The analysis of concept representations and the possibility of control distillation offer new avenues for research.
    * The ablations and hyperparameter analyses help in understanding the robustness of the method.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The hybrid approach requires loading both base and distilled models, increasing memory requirements, which can be a limitation in some resource-constrained environments. While they address this with an alternative method of skipping the first timestep, this method is shown to have some limitations.
    *   The improvement in diversity is primarily evaluated using visual inspection and DreamSim distance. While these are reasonable metrics, more sophisticated metrics that capture semantic diversity could strengthen the evaluation.
    * The paper focuses mostly on the visual quality and diversity aspect. An analysis on how this impacts editing quality would be valuable.
    * Although promising, the scalability of the method to other distillation techniques or diffusion architectures could be explored further.

**Overall:**

The paper is a significant contribution to the field of diffusion model distillation. It provides a novel analysis technique, a practical solution to a critical problem, and interesting insights into the representational properties of distilled models. While some limitations exist, the strengths of the paper outweigh its weaknesses. The insights into diversity during timesteps has a potential to have a significant impact, and thus, this work is valuable.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.GR
- **Score**: 8/10

### Studying Classifier(-Free) Guidance From a Classifier-Centric Perspective
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10638v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaoming Zhao, Alexander G. Schwing
- **Abstract**: Classifier-free guidance has become a staple for conditional generation with denoising diffusion models. However, a comprehensive understanding of classifier-free guidance is still missing. In this work, we carry out an empirical study to provide a fresh perspective on classifier-free guidance. Concretely, instead of solely focusing on classifier-free guidance, we trace back to the root, i.e., classifier guidance, pinpoint the key assumption for the derivation, and conduct a systematic study to understand the role of the classifier. We find that both classifier guidance and classifier-free guidance achieve conditional generation by pushing the denoising diffusion trajectories away from decision boundaries, i.e., areas where conditional information is usually entangled and is hard to learn. Based on this classifier-centric understanding, we propose a generic postprocessing step built upon flow-matching to shrink the gap between the learned distribution for a pre-trained denoising diffusion model and the real data distribution, majorly around the decision boundaries. Experiments on various datasets verify the effectiveness of the proposed approach.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Studying Classifier(-Free) Guidance From a Classifier-Centric Perspective":

**Summary:**

The paper investigates classifier-free guidance (CFG), a popular technique in denoising diffusion models for conditional generation, from a classifier-centric viewpoint. Instead of solely focusing on CFG, the authors analyze classifier guidance (CG), its origin, and identify a key assumption in its derivation. They demonstrate that both CG and CFG work by pushing denoising trajectories away from decision boundaries where conditional information is entangled. Based on this understanding, the paper proposes a generic post-processing step using flow matching to reduce the gap between the learned distribution and the real data distribution, particularly around the decision boundaries. Experiments on various datasets validate the proposed approach's effectiveness.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty (6/10):** The paper's novelty lies in its *perspective* rather than introducing entirely new algorithms. Examining CFG through the lens of its parent technique, CG, is a valuable contribution. The identification of the "pushing away from decision boundaries" mechanism and the subsequent post-processing strategy are incremental but insightful. While the flow matching technique itself is not new, its specific application to *correct* samples produced by CFG/CG diffusion models – particularly around decision boundaries – constitutes a meaningful adaptation. The experiments validating this view point make the paper stronger.
*   **Significance (6/10):** Understanding the *why* behind a technique as widely used as CFG is crucial. By clarifying the underlying mechanism and addressing limitations, the paper provides insights that can guide future research and potentially improve conditional generation. It could lead to better fine-tuning strategies for CFG, more targeted architectures for conditional diffusion models, or new ways to improve fidelity in conditional generation. However, the improvement is incremental and the approach has limitations that prevent it from being truly significant.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear and well-structured:** The paper is easy to follow, clearly explaining the motivation, methodology, and experimental results.
    *   **Empirical validation:** Extensive experiments on synthetic and real datasets support the authors' claims.
    *   **Practical implications:** The proposed post-processing step offers a readily implementable method for improving the fidelity of CFG-based generation.
    *   **Well researched:** The paper builds on previous work and gives a clear picture on the current literature.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Incremental improvement:** The proposed post-processing step, while effective, offers an incremental improvement over existing CFG approaches. The impact is also hard to gauge from the provided results, as it's hard to see with the naked eye what is improved.
    *   **Computational overhead:** The added post-processing step does increase computational cost, which is an existing limitation to diffusion models.
    *   **Limited insights from synthetic experiments:** While informative, the use of idealized synthetic data might not fully reflect the complexities of real-world datasets.
    *   **Limited exploration of potential issues:** The paper may not completely examine potential issues related to bias amplification if the classifier is biased itself.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence the field by:

    *   Encouraging researchers to consider a more classifier-centric approach to analyzing and improving CFG and related techniques.
    *   Spawning new post-processing methods that specifically target decision boundary regions.
    *   Motivating the development of more robust and less biased classifiers for guiding diffusion models.

**Justification for Score:**

The score of 6/10 reflects the paper's incremental, yet valuable, contributions. The change in viewpoint from classifier-free guidance to classifier-centric is refreshing and it allows us to view things through another lens. It is well-executed and provides insightful analysis. The paper isn't groundbreaking in terms of new algorithms or theoretical breakthroughs, but it is clearly written and methodologically sound. It contributes to the community's understanding of conditional generation with diffusion models. However, it suffers from the improvement not being that visually clear, and a lack of exploration of possible issues the approach may have.

**Score: 6**

### GoT: Unleashing Reasoning Capability of Multimodal Large Language Model for Visual Generation and Editing
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10639v1)
- **Authors**: Rongyao Fang, Chengqi Duan, Kun Wang, Linjiang Huang, Hao Li, Shilin Yan, Hao Tian, Xingyu Zeng, Rui Zhao, Jifeng Dai, Xihui Liu, Hongsheng Li
- **Abstract**: Current image generation and editing methods primarily process textual prompts as direct inputs without reasoning about visual composition and explicit operations. We present Generation Chain-of-Thought (GoT), a novel paradigm that enables generation and editing through an explicit language reasoning process before outputting images. This approach transforms conventional text-to-image generation and editing into a reasoning-guided framework that analyzes semantic relationships and spatial arrangements. We define the formulation of GoT and construct large-scale GoT datasets containing over 9M samples with detailed reasoning chains capturing semantic-spatial relationships. To leverage the advantages of GoT, we implement a unified framework that integrates Qwen2.5-VL for reasoning chain generation with an end-to-end diffusion model enhanced by our novel Semantic-Spatial Guidance Module. Experiments show our GoT framework achieves excellent performance on both generation and editing tasks, with significant improvements over baselines. Additionally, our approach enables interactive visual generation, allowing users to explicitly modify reasoning steps for precise image adjustments. GoT pioneers a new direction for reasoning-driven visual generation and editing, producing images that better align with human intent. To facilitate future research, we make our datasets, code, and pretrained models publicly available at https://github.com/rongyaofang/GoT.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "GoT: Unleashing Reasoning Capability of Multimodal Large Language Model for Visual Generation and Editing":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Generation Chain-of-Thought (GoT), a novel paradigm for image generation and editing that integrates reasoning into the process.  Instead of directly mapping textual prompts to images, GoT first generates a step-by-step reasoning chain in natural language, describing semantic relationships and spatial arrangements. This reasoning chain then guides the image generation or editing process. The authors define a specific formulation for GoT, emphasizing semantic and spatial information.  They construct a large-scale GoT dataset (over 9 million samples) to train their models. The paper proposes a unified framework based on the Qwen2.5-VL model for reasoning and a diffusion model enhanced by a Semantic-Spatial Guidance Module (SSGM). Experiments demonstrate improved performance in both text-to-image generation and image editing compared to existing methods. GoT also enables interactive generation where users can modify the reasoning steps.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of integrating chain-of-thought reasoning, common in LLMs, into image generation is novel.  While prior works have used LLMs for layout planning or text encoding in image generation, this paper takes a step further by having the model explicitly reason about the scene's semantic structure and spatial relationships *before* generation.  The creation of a large-scale, spatially-annotated dataset tailored to this approach is also a significant contribution. Formulating GOT with spatial components and the SSGM module also adds value.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a key limitation in current image generation models: the lack of explicit reasoning about scene composition.  By introducing a reasoning-guided framework, the authors pave the way for more controllable and interpretable image generation. The improved performance on standard benchmarks, along with the demonstration of interactive generation, highlight the potential of this approach.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear problem definition:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing methods in handling complex scenes and spatial arrangements.
    *   **Novel approach:**  The GoT paradigm offers a conceptually different approach to image generation.
    *   **Extensive dataset:** The creation of a large-scale GoT dataset is a substantial undertaking and valuable resource for the community.
    *   **Strong experimental results:** The quantitative and qualitative results demonstrate the effectiveness of the GoT framework. The ablations clearly validate the design choices.
    *   **Interactive generation:** The interactive generation capability is a significant advantage, allowing users to control the generation process.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dependence on Qwen2.5-VL:** The framework is tightly coupled with the Qwen2.5-VL model.  While Qwen2.5-VL is a strong baseline, the results are tied to its specific architecture and capabilities. The generalizability of the GoT paradigm to other MLLMs needs further investigation.
    *   **Limited exploration of alternative reasoning architectures:** The paper focuses on a specific formulation of GoT, and may be limited in the way it addresses compositionality compared to how humans think.
    *   **Computational cost:** The construction of GoT datasets required a lot of computation. Even with that and the low-rank adaptions, the system complexity (MLLM + diffusion) likely adds significant computational overhead to both training and inference. Further practical analysis would be valuable.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in image generation and editing by:
    *   Encouraging the development of more reasoning-driven models.
    *   Providing a framework for controllable and interpretable generation.
    *   Promoting the use of large-scale datasets with spatial annotations.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of image generation and editing. The GoT paradigm offers a promising approach to addressing the limitations of existing methods, and the results are compelling. While there are some limitations regarding the dependence on a specific MLLM and computational complexity, the strengths of the paper outweigh the weaknesses. This framework opens up new research avenues for more controllable and interpretable visual synthesis.

**Score: 8**
- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10
